COLLISION IN FOG.

Alarming Rzilway Accident near Sankey.

PASSENGERS STRAN DED.

Consterpation was caused in Warrington on Monday evening 'by rumours that a disastrous accident had occurred on the Cheshire Lines Railway, near Sankey. Fortunately the affair die not turn out to be as serious as was at first represented. Nevertheless, two persons suffered injury, and for some hours there was an entire suspension of traffic on the main line between Liverpool and Manchester and the Widnes branch line. The railway runs direct between the two cities, but there are loops into Warrington and Widnes for local trains. A train from Widnes reached the main line about 5.30, precisely at the moment that the 4.40 goods train from Liver- pool reached the spot. It was very foggy. The two trains, approaching on diagonally converging lines and travelling in the same direction, struck each other diagonally. They jostled against each other for some distance, tearing up the permanent way and each threatening to overturn as well as derail the other. It was the goods train, as it fortunately happened, that had the least power of resistance. It was thrown off the line and several of the waggons fell over the embankment into the fields below. A scene of great confusion followed. Cattle trucks, with moaning cattle in them, were strewn about the line and in the fields, and the difficulty of dealing with the accident was increased by the train having carried the telegraph poles away and interrupted communica- tion with Warrington and Widnes. No one was hurt in the passenger train, but the driver, Daniel Downey, of Northcote-road, Walton, Liverpool, was taken to the Warrington Infirmary and detained suffering from concussion of the brain. The fireman was also slightly hurt. Both lines were blocked, and all traffic between Man- chester and Liverpool was stopped. One effect of the accident was to leave dozens of passengers stranded at Widnes for the night. They had booked through to other places but were unable to complete the journey. The work of opening up communication was accom- plished considerably after midnight, and the train which leaves Liverpool daily t : 5.30 reached the Central Station. Manchester, at hatfTpast one o'clock on Tuesday morning. One of the worst features of the accident was that hourly trains were despatched from Liverpool Central Station and then held up on the lines. Scores of people were thus imprisoned in railway carriages, cold and hungry, for five, six and seven hourc.. iLoud complaints have been made since as this piece of .cruel mismanage- ment.

CROSFIELD'S v. "DAILY MAIL."

A Settlement

In Monday's `1 Daily Mail " and " Daily Mirror " the following appeared : "THE SOAP-MAKERS' COMBINATION. We are glad to be able to state that the differences which existed between ourselves and Messrs. Joseph Crostield and Sons, Ltd., the well-known soap-makers,of Warrington, and which resulted in legal proceedinbs, have been satisfactorily settled. Messrs. Crosfield, while not contesting our right to criticise the soap-makers' combination last year, have considered that certain paragraphs in the articles which were then printed might be understood as conveying imputations upon their personal honour and commercial integrity. While we ara glad to have this opportunity of stating that no such imputations were intended, we willingly withdraw any remark which might be so interpreted." ASK YOUR GROCER FOR ivicDOUGALL'S Paterit SELF—RAISING FLOUR The Examiner, Saturday December 28, 1907, -WO M AN'S REALM. SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS. PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS

VEGETABLE MONSTROSITIES..

- --- Animals which depart in a striking degree from the normal forms of their species usually excite astonishment, pity, or disgust. On the other hand, as Dr. Gbel, in a study of vegetable monstrosities, points out, the same sentiments are not aroused by similar eccentricities in the vegetable world. In fact, vegetable monstrosities are relatively so com- mon that they pass unnoticed except by botanists. Moreover, in all times they have been purposely encouraged and cultivated. The Japanese are experts in this regard. Dr. ebel concludes that vegetable monstrosi- ties, in accordance with the discoveries of De Vries concerning sudden mutations, are due to the development, under special excitation, of latent characters, which possess in them- selves no utility in the species in which they exist.

" A youthful aspirant for journalistic distinction. who was asked to write an article on superstition and imbecility, began his essay thus :—"That imbecility is not on the wane, perusal of the hollowing lines will amply demonstrate."

PUBLIC POLICY 'AND MARRIAGE.

There Care many well-meaning people who do not understand why a promise of marriage made by a married man should not be enforceable when the man is in a position to carry it out. We would strongly advise them, says the Quecn, read, mark, learn, and inwardly digest the judgment of Mr. Justice Phillimore in the case of "Spicer v. Hunt," as reported in the Times. His Lordship is to be heartily thanked by all who uphold the sanctity of marriage for his fear- less and able reading of the law applicable to this case. Although there have been two other cases recently in which the same point has arisen for consideration, Mr. Justice Phillimore alone seems to have been able to take a view which must commend itself to all men of intelligence and common sense, and is also in accord with the fixed principles of law, which refuse to sanc- tion agreements which are contrary to public policy. To hold such a contract valid," says the American Chief Justice Laurence, "would intro- duce into social life a dangerous and immoral principle. Only in the most corrupt conditions of society &rad such agreements be tolerated as lawful. They are themselves a violation of mar- riage duties, and the persons who make them are morally unfaithful to their marriage ties. Such contracts are deeply at war with the best interests of social life.' An eminent English Chancery Judge, Lgrd Justice Knight Bruce, again, held strongly the view that the tendency of such a contract was to make the husband in thought, if not in deed, unfaithful to his wife, and in certain cases it might even lead to crime, while its probable result was immorality. Our law has always discountenanced any ar- rangement which might interfere with the pre- servation inviolate of the closest relations be- ',men husband and wife, and discouraged every ..ransaction the tendency of which was to give the husband another woman to care for as well as or instead of his wife, and vice versa. Mr. Justice -Phillimore has always set a very high i standard of public and private morality, and n his latest exposition of the law has laid down a principle which will be acceptable to all who hold that the foundation of every stable society must be the maintenance in its integrity of the mar- riage *tate

HALLEY'S COMET.

Halley's domet is in its name a perpetual tribute to the genius of an English astrono- mer. For one of Halley's most notable achievements was the calculation of the orbit of .the 1682 comet, the first ever calculated. Finding that its elements were nearly the same as those of comets which had appeared in 1531 and 1607, he concluded that all three orbits belonged to the same comet. Its period was found to be about seventy-six years, and Halley boldly predicted its return in 1757. And although the astronomer did not live to see it, the comet duly appeared that year, reaching perihelion in 1759. Halley's comet had been observed as early as 1066, when it is. said to have appeared tour times as large as Venus, and to have shone with a light equal to a quarter that of the mobil. From this up to 1456 there is no historical record of its appearance. Its appearance in 1835 was well observed in almost every astronomical observatory.

HALLEY'S COMET AND MAY METEORS..

Astronomers are generally agreed in con- Edering that many meteor swarms are the remains of lost comets, .of which the no longer incandescent matter still goes coursing round the sun in elliptic orbits. The meteors of the latter part of November, for example, are identified with Biela's lost comet. It is pcssible. however, says a Globe writer., that a still living comet may give rise to a meteor swarm. And the great similarity in the orbits of the May meteors known as Aquarids, and of Halley's comet, has led to the suggestion that the meteor swarm and the comet are parts of the same system revolving round the sun. This leads to the conception of a comet's orbit as' more or less occupied by matter through its entire length. In parts where the meteoric matter is thickly aggre- gated frequent collisions give rise to inpan- descence and form the comet. Behind this follows a, long train of sparsely aggregated matter, fragments of which may be drawn into our atmosphere as meteors.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPTICAL LEVER.

The delicate measurements demanded by modern scientific processes and machines have led to the invention of many instru- ments of precision that surprise the uniniti- .ted by their Capabilities, which are often “ased on extremely simple principles. Among ,ese is a little apparatus put upon the mar- ket in France for determining the thickness, or, as one might say, lack of thickness, of ex- tremely thin plates, wires, and threads. The inventor liatens its action to that of a lever in which a ray of light takes the place of the beam. Essentially the apparatus consists of two carefully-ground plates in contact with one another, the upper one being attached to a movable arm. When an object is placed be- tween the edges of the plates the upper plate is displaced a little in level, and the effect of this displacement is magnified by a reflected ray of light which falls upon a graduated scale. Thus the most delicate measurements of thickness are easily and quickly made.

VICISSITUDES OF A CABLE.

The vicissitudes of a submarine cable are many, says Mr. C. Bright in the Magazine of Commerce. It may be torn by an anchor, cruhcl by a rock, or seriously damaged by a coral reef, such,. as abound in the tropics. Some of the growths often found on a cable tend to gradually decay the iron sheathing wires. Then again, a cable is sometimes severed- by a seaquake. It may be fatally at- tacked by the snout of a eaw.fish, or by the spike of a swordfish. But perhaps the little animal that makes itself mcst objectionable, from the cable engineer's, standpoint, is the insignificent7looking teredo navalis. This little beast is

INTENSELY GREEDY

where gutta-percha is concerned, working its way there between the iron wires and be- tween the serving yarns. The silica in the outer cable compound tends to defeat the teredo's efforts at making a meal off the core; and this defeat is further . effected by the serVed core being enveloped in a thin taping of brass. But where the bottom is known to be badly infected with these little monsters of the deep, the insulator is 'often composed of indiarubber, which has no attractions for the reclo, and possesses a toughness, moreover, ./liich is less 'suited for its boring tool than the comparatively cheeselike guttapercha. From one cause or another, faults occur in most cables from time to time. These require to be .

When the cable has really been. hooked and picked up—an operation which may entail several weeks, or even months, if only in waitin for favourable weather—the bight is secured at the boWs, and afterwards cut. Each end is then brought on board al- ternately and tested electrically. If found to be sound, the necessary. repairs are then effected.

EFFECT OF RADIUM RAYS ON JEWELS.

The Comptes Pen des has published papers on the -effect of radium rays on quartz, fluor- spar, and the various 'forms of native alu- mina. One is by Berthelot, who found that it is impossible to impart a colour to colour- less quartz and to white fluorspar unless it contains traces of manganese. A specimen of white cleavable fluorspar chewed no colouring in twelve months. A violet amethyst quartz, on the other hand, Could be made colourless heating, owing probably to the reduction of manganese peroxide, and the colour was restored by exposure to radium rays,. which reoxidised the manganese. Traces of the latter were revealed by chemical analysis.. A remarkable -observatien was that if colourless fluorspar is left for some time in a solution of manganese acetate. ;:and then exposed to radium rays, the interior of the crystal. •

GRADUALLY BECAME ROSE-COLOURED. This is all the more curious, as a saturated solution of the acetate or chloride of man- ganese is not affected by radium rays. Berthe- lot thinks that the manganese is peroxidised by radium rays absorbed in the interior of the crystal, and then diffuses through it. Another case observed was that of a green Tyrolese emerald., which lest colour on heat- ing. In this case the colouring was probably due to carbides of hydrogen, which were de- stroyed by heating. and not •restored -by radium rays.

The tiecond paper is a second communica- tion from F. Boras, who has improved his process by surrounding crystals with radium and turning them from time to time so as to ensure uniformity act colouring. In this man- ner, he

GOT' BEAUTIFUL COLOURINGS in a few days. the strength of the radium used being 1.800.000. Beads of colourless ilumina were thus coloured pink or sherry colour. An important fact elicited ,by the author is that this action goes on undisturbed even at, a temperature of --200 deg. C. We know that at that temperature all- known chemical actions are very sluggish, and so this observation would tend to make Berthe- lot's hypothesis of a chemical action im- orobable. To thig it may. of course. be oh. jected that this action of radium, being un- • usually effective at ordinary temperatures, may be altogether independent of temperattue.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Christmas in Germany.—ln no other country in the world is Christmas so generally and so enthusiastically celebrated as in Germany, nor is there any other land where Christmas customs, which had their origin in a far remote past, have been so carefully and religiously observed. The German not only sends his toys to the British market, but keeps a great abundance of them wherewith to decorate the Christmas tree which is a cherished institution in every home, and to supplement for the delectation of the younger members of the family, the presents which are arranged on the table beneath the tree. The marriage of Queen Victoria to a German Prince was the means of bringing to us many German customs, and among them the Christmas tree, but stay-at-home British folks can have little idea of the exteht to which .these trees enter into the cele- brations of the German Christmas. Whatever one may think of the Germans at other times—and they possess many solid good qualities which claim our admiration —it is impossible to regard otherwise than with interest the loyalty and devotion, varying .sometimes upon the pathetic, with which even the poorest observe at this season of the year, the national customs. In whatever other direction it is necessary to economise,each family must have its Christmas tree, and where" res angusta dome " prohibit the purchase of real tree, art comes to the rescue, with an inexpensive substitute called a "pyramid "which has at any rate the advantage that it can be employed again and again. New Year Gifts in Franca—With us the custom of giving Christmas presents is much more popular than the giving of New ear Gifts, but in France it is the other way about. In that country it has always been the custom to reserve presents for the New Year, and they are called ctrennes.—Lady Crewe, Lord Roseberry's daughter, the Lady Peggy Primrose who has been it figure of interest since girlhood, bears the quaint name of Etrenne. She keeps her birthday on the first of January, and this name was bestowed upon her in sympathy with the French custom and to mark her coming as a New Year's Gift to her parents. Lord and Lady Dudley.—Lord and Lady Dudley's little twin sons are both fine healthy boys, and it is understood that the King and Prince of Wales will act as sponsors at the christening which is to take place early in the New Year. Lady Dudley was Miss Rachel Guraey, and married Lord Dudley in 1891. She comes of the well-known Quaker family of Norfolk bankers, and when financial troubles overtook her father, was adopted by Adeline, Duchess of Bedford, who intro- duced her into society. The wedding was one of the leading Society events of the time. Lord Dudley came into his title and property in 1885 and he owns large estates in Jamaica, when he and Lady Dudley were staying at the time of the recent terrible earthquake in that island. _ _

The New Royal Yacht.—The new Royal yacht which has been bnilt on the Clyde, is sxpected to be ready for the use of the King and Queen early in the Spring. She has been named the "Alexandra,"and is intended to be more of a private family yacht than is the larger and more costly " Victoria and Albert." Her light draught will enable her to enter ports such as Nice and others,which are not open to the "Victoria and Albert" drawing a larger depth of water. But although smaller, the "Alexandreis fitted with turbine engines,and all the latest improvements. She has a fine bridge deck some 150 feet long, and comfort and convenience, rather than magnificence, have been studied in the furnishing of the saloons and cabins and in the general appointments. Feminine Letter-Writing and Stationery.—Experte say they can tell a womans age by her handwriting, and style of correspondence, bnt although this must not be taken too literally, the late Queen's correspondence and her habit of underlining, illustrate the great changes that have taken place in feminine letter-writing in the past two or three generations. The delicate sloping hand has entirely disappeared, and so has the habit of underlining. Women's writing has become by slow degrees more masculine than feminine until now there is little to distinguish between the two, unless it is that the woman's hand is generally more clear and legible. And we have too a much better class of writing materials than had our mothers and grandmothers of the Victorian period. As regards stationery, fashions are constantly changing, and only white, cream, and grey notepaper is now used. All.g.howy shades of colour are out of favour so also is the til,e of crests, and elaborate moco.grams, but a fashion of the moment is to write one's initials in the top left hand corner of the envelope A Cosy Idea.—Theatre muffs are being provided for the aftluent this winter, sometimes built of material to match the coat or cloak, but more generally of hand- painted chiffon over white satin, and ornamented with frills of lace, deep silk fringe, tassels, ropes and ends of chenille, or bugles. In shape these muffs resemble a huge sachet, and contain little pockets for handker- chief, powder-puff, smelling salts, &c Exquisite examples again are carried out in artificial flowers, so , realistic and so perfectly scented as to de-clove all but the closest observers. With the trails of blooms and foliage, such muffs resemble a bouquet, and are usually rethined by their owners during the evening. About muffs.—lt is difficult to realise that once upon a time men carried muffs, yet the fact remains that about the year 1680, huge muffs—generally of leopard skin, and uttached to a silken cord which was passed round the neck of the wearer, were adopted by the sterner sex. This was some 200 years after the muff first saw light in Venice,when it was exactly the reverse of what it is at the present moment, being very small, and covered with a rich and costly brocade, the fur being used as a Ening, instead of appearing outside. Towards the end of the seventeenll2 century, white skins dyed pink, blue, or green, were utilised for the muffs of fashionable women, and it is a little surprising that this particular fashion has not seen the light again in these days of seeking after curious and weird efieeta. r Disastrous Colour Schemes.—As might have been supposed the craze for complicated colour schemes is spelling disastrous results In the hands of less gifted milliners and dressmakers and not infrequently we come face to face with combinations that set our nerves upon edge. Now of all the things the woman who is compelled to dress upon a slender allowance should aim at, is simplicity, both in style and colorings. Plain and simple should be the motto of the impecunious, leavin; elaboration to those who can afford to pay " shining lights "to successfully carry it out. The Passing of Powerful Scents.—Among fashionable women—which leaves about them traces of an indes- cribable, sweet, fresh, wholesome perfume—is taking the pldce of bath powders, such as eau-de-Cologne, or lavender powders. This is no doubt owing to the strong feeling against the more powerful perfumes, which for sachets, placing in the linings of dress receptacles and spiinkling upon handkerchiefs, are now severely set aside. Scents for toilet purposes were first used by the ancient Egyptians, but it was the Romans who introduced them into these islands. They found that the Saxons did not know of them, although they employed spices and odoriferona woods. Rose water did not come into fashion until the Middle Ages, and then the Court Beauties took to it eagerly, and would not travel without carrying sufficient with them in which to wash their hands and faces during the period Of their absence from home.

HINTS FOR THE HOME.

To revive the lustre of morocco, or any other leather, apply the white of an egg with a sponge.

A cup of milk added to the water with which an oilcloth or oiled floor is to be washed gives lustre -like new.

To clean varnished wall-paper, use the following mixture. Add half a pint of paraffin to a pailful of warm war. • Wash the walls with a woollen cloth, wrung out fairly dry, and polish with a dry linen cloth.

To renovate leather chair covers, rub the leather first with a little hot milk. Melt some beeswax in hot water, add sufficient turpentine to make it the consistency of thin cream. Rub this on the covers, and polish with a soft cloth.

" Vanity Bag" is a necessity to every woman, an 3 consists of a small receptacle containing cold cream, hair and hat pins, fringe nets, a spare veil, a papier poudre book, a tiny smelling salts, minia- ture mirror, brush and comb, and bottle of scent.

WASHING WHITE GLOVES. If your white washing gloves have black points, before washing them slightly moisten a little salt, and with your finger rub it well into the points. This will prevent the black from running, which . otherwise it is very liable to do.

HEALTH AND DIETARY.

There is evidence of a manifest desire on the part of this generation to live more wisely than was the custom of their ancestors. The in- ct eased 164revity of the last half-century is probably due as much to diminished eating, says the Hospital, as to any other of the many single causes to which it has been attributed. The man who every . day partakes of hors d'ceurres, and soup, and fish, and entrées, and a joint, and game or some substitute for it, and sweets. and dessert, has likewise given an atytepta iee, and will certainly some day be called upon to honour it on demand. The vagaries of vege- tarianism or other fanciful plans of diet resoive themselves into this, that it is essential to health to eat moderately, to masticate the food, and not to eat too much. If these rules be observed, the character of what is eaten may be regarded as 'a matter of comparatively small importanee.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
FRUITS DIGEST FCODS

Apart from the fact that fruits contain certain salts which build up the tissues of the body, they also directly aid in enriching the blood with the iron of the more solid food stuffs. It is also pointed out by Dr. J. Gordon Sharp that they actually digest our nitrogenous foods. If the white of egg be placed in a glass dish between layers of fresh, ripe strawberries cut into slices, digestion will take place in about eighb hours. Similar experiments were conducted with ripe cherries, and a dry extract of these was found active at the end of two years. Orange juice also proved to be a digestive, as did pear and apple Juice. Tinned fruits are inert as digestives. The prolonged high temperature necessary to their preservation destroys the ferments.

HINTS FOR FUR RENOVATION.

There is hardly anything more expensive than ft.r working. and yet people are often Trite afraid to try and se* fur at home, although it is not really 'half so troublesome as at first sight appears. With a little patience and knowledge and a good fur needle the difficulties are soon surmounted. Of course everything• has to be done from the back, and if those who wear beautiful fur garments ever happen to locrk at the rear they will be quite astonished to see the infinitesimal seams _which denote so much patience and perseverance on the part of the worker. It is rather a daring thing to under- take to transform a fur garment, but with a good pattern it is to be done. A short sleeveless bolero can with time and ingenuity be turned into a stole, and a very presentable stole too, with the addition of fur, tails, which with a little hunting up can be bought without any wonder- ful expense ; but this is one of the things where there is such an enormous difference in the price when it becomes a question of where to buy. It is generally an improvement to a fur to clean it a little before starting on the renovations, and this is best done by rubbing flour well into it and well out. It is quite a4etonishing how dirty the flour will become. It is a good thing to hang fufs out Of doors, and to comb them well with a metal comb, which can be bought for a penny; this will take away any chance of moths being hidden in it. This wants to be carefully done, and every inch of the fur gone over, shak- ing it well from time to "time. A little beating does no harm. This brings back its lustre. If the fur is very dirty indeed, hot sea sand will cleanse it better than flour, but this applies to dark furs; flour is best for light grey and white ones. They require to be lightly treated, for it is easy to tear the skin. To cut the skin you want a sharp penknife, then pull the fur aria,:. It must never be cut with scissors. You mu ,t not overlap the fur in sewing seams; the Macs are placed together and held firmly in o!acte and sewn over and over again. I.4inen thread is ut•ed by furriers, and sometimes this is waxed. Avoid silk, for it cuts the leather.

SOME GOOD GROUSE RECIPES.

ROAST GROUSE.—PIuck a brace of grouse and wipe them thoroughly, inside and out, with a damp cloth. Mix ioz. of butter with a little black pepper and salt, a few drops of lemon. juice, and place a small lump inside each bird. Truss for roasting, and dredge the outside with flour. Wrap one or two vine leaves and a piece of fat bacon over the breast, and place at once before the fire or in the oven. Allow twenty- five to thirty minutes for a young bird. Make a slice of thick buttered toast, and when the birds are done lay them on this, garnish with watercress, and serve with browned breadcrumbs and bread sauce.

CHARTREUSE OF GROUSE.—Preparo a brace of grouse for cooking, larding the breasts. Put ilb. of butter, in a stewpan, and, when it is smok- ing hot, brown the grouse on both sides. When they have taken a good colour, add half a glass of white wine and the same quantity of stock. Add some pepper and salt, a pinch of mixed herbs, a sliced carrot, and two onions, and allow all to cook together. When the grouse are ready, remove them, and stew till tender in the same sauce one or two whole carrots and tuinips, and lettuces or cabbage. Then cut the carrots and turnips into neat rounds, have ready a buttered mould, line it with pieces of the stewed grouse, between each strip place a round of carrot and turnip. Make a layer of the same at the bot- tom of the mould, cover with another of the stewed lettuce. and fill up with cooked peas and French bean,, placing a layer of lettuce last of all. Steam for a few moments, and turn out, serving the sauce round it.

COLD GROUSE WITH MAYONNAISE SAUCE.-Cut I. the Cold grouse into four pieces, lay them on an-I entree dish. Take four dessertspoonfuls of salad oil, three spoonfuls of savoury calves' foot jelly, ! two spoonfuls of vinegar, one- or two shallots 1 §nely minced, one or two chopped capers, a ! little pepper and salt. Stir well till the sauce has thickened, pour it over the cold grouse, and ; serve with a salad of lettuce and cucumber.

- GROUSE PlE.—Pick and draw two ' brace of young 'oTouse, divide each WM into, four pieces. fry till half-cooked in a. little butter, and season with cayenne, mace, salt, and pepper. In another, pan lightly fry some raw minced beef, and season with chopped shallots and mush- rooms, a little salt and pepper. Phice in a pie dish. Lay the quarters of grouse on the top. garnish with one or two hard-boiled eggs, cut in rings, fill up the dish with a good stock, strengthened with meat essence. Cover with a good paste, and bake an hour and a-quarter to an hour and a-half.

GROUSE SAUTES.—Take a brace and a-half of young grouse, pluck them, and prepare them 7 for cooking. C.';ut them into joints, put a little! butter in a stewpan, and saute the pieces over a quick fire, adding sit and pepper to taste. When cooked through, piactio them in another saucepan at the side of the fire to keep hot. i Make a good sauce by stirring a little flour into the butter in which the grouse was cooked. add- ing by degrees equal quantities of white wine and good stock. Stir well while the sauce boils up, continuing to stir till it has reduced. When , it is of a right consistency, season to taste, add ! a squeeze of lemon juice, place the grcuse on an entree .dish, pour the sauce over, and. serve nu- I mediately.—Erening Standard.

RAILWAY TABLE.

THE STORY OF

This has been pre-emine it has seen industrial

scientific application and fresh energy. It has been the year of The Hague Conference and of the historic luncheon at Windsor Castle—eight crowned heads round one board; It has brought the second Imperial Confer- ence, to make the Mother Country better understand the aspirations of her children,. A couple of threatened wars in the industrial realm have been averted by the tact of the President of the Board of Trade. The ex- pansion of commerce has gone on steadily. Exploration and research have enlarged the bounds of knowledge. The velocity of the Atlantic leviathans; Arctic and Antarctic discoveries; the reduction of wireless tele- graphy to terms of £ s. d.; motoring develop- ments; new exploits with balloons and with flying machines; the maritime use of the gyroscope; and a cycle of national exhibi- tions—these things have all helped to em- phasise how the arts may flourish in time of peace.

THE KING'S SAGACIOUS SERVICES.

The movements of King Edward have been directed to one end —of promoting inter- national amity. Iris Majesty has twice within the twelve months called: on Presi- dent Fallieres. He visited the King of Italy at Gaeta in the spring; received Prince Fushimi in May and the King of Siam in June.; proceeded with the German Kaiser to Wilhelmshoe in August; and met at Ischl in the same month the Emperor of Austria— who has lately, amid general rejoicings, en- tered upon the celebration of a diamond jubilee.- King Edward also entertained at Marienbad M. Clemenceau (the French Pre- mier), and M. Isvolsky, the Russian Minister' of Foreign Affairs. Of his Majesty's, many home engagements, the most prominent were the visit with Queen Alexandra to Ireland, and the ,opening of Cardiff's new dock, when a working man Lord Mayor was created a knight. Even more charmingly than as guest did King Edward figure in the role of host. The -company et the unique luncheon-party at Windsor on November 17th included the Kaiser and Kaiserin, the Queen of Norway, the King and Queen of Spain, the Queen of Portugal, the Prince and Princess of Wales, and the Grand Duke and Grand Duchess Vladimir of Russia. This was the most pic- turesque and significant Royal event of the year; but the record given so far by no means accounts for all the Royal guests who _have been welcomed. The Queen's sister, the Dowager-Empress of Russia, was here in 'March; the King and Queen of Denmark came and were entertained at the' Guildhall in June; and in November at Wood Norton there was an assemblage of Franco-Iberian Royal personages for the. r wedding of the Queen of Portugal's sister (a sister, too, of the Due d'Orleans) with Prince Charles of Bourbon, brother-in-law of the King of Spain. It should be added to this résumé of movements that the Czar and the German Kaiser met at Swinemunde early in August; and that the King and Queen of Norway paid a state visit to Paris in May. WHAT THE HAGUE CONFERENCE DID.

The evicted tenants of Ireland have some- thing to be thankful for. As new Irish Secre- tary Mr. Birrell has done well for them. The Estates Commisaioners are to get them land, by compulsory means, on which they will be set up. Mr. Birrell's second egg was addled. Whitsuntide brought the unanimous rejection of his Irish Council Bill by • the Irish National Convention Mr. McKenna's gducatlion . Bill embodied some of the non-contentious clauses of the shipwrecked measure of a twelvemonth ago. These provide for the medical inspection of 1 children in elementary schools, for the pro- vision of play centres, and for vacation schools. Non-county Boroughs and Urban District Councils are permitted to appro- priate land acquired for other objeets to the purposes of higher education. County Coun- cils, too, may promote the multiplication of such schools by capital contributions. Women may now be elected to Town and County Councils and become, aldermen and mayors, though if ever :they become Mayors they are not to be magistrates lex officio. A separate Act extends the new qualification to the area of Scotland: The Deceased Wife's Sister Bill, after THE IMPERIAL CONFERENCE AND many years' vicissitudes, was made an Act of Parliament. So much upon the social side. The business side is represented by the Peace Conference at The Hague. The work actually done, falls principally under three heads: First, the Prize Court emerges _ from a shadowy possibility to a substantial reality. The , previous Permanent Court of Arbitra- tion was a mere list of available judges. In future the Prize Court of Appeal will be a working bench of fifteen judges, elected by the forty-six Powers, with conditions for appeal in times of war defined. There are not, however, any universally acknowledged laws of maritime war. England will call a conference here of the nine naval Powers next year to discuss a naval 'code. The time was found to be not ripe for obligatory arbi- tration, and the scheme for a Court of A ri-li- tral Justice was wrecked by the South Ai can States, which demanded the same voting weight as the great Powers in the eletion of judges. The foundation-stone of a permanent Palace of Peace was laid.

SIR. 'HALDANE'S TERRITORIAL FORCES. Mr. Haldane's' Territorial and Reserve Forces Act should stand to his credit in his- tory. It welds Militia, Volunteers, and Ireo- Maury into one territorial force under County Associations. Over these associations, whose work will be administrative, the Lords- Lieutenant of the county will preside. The Associations will consist of officers, co-opted members, and representatives of local authorities. The Associations will hold land, will provision and maintain rifle ranges, will consider areas for manceuvres, will form cadet corps (though no Parliamentary money is to be available for youths under sixteen), will-_provide homes, and will do something for reservists and discharged soldiers. Men will serve four years, and may be " re-engaged " for a further term of Jour years. They will be exempt from jury service. Anyone may buy discharge for RS—except when the Territorial Force is being called out ror permanent service. That emnodiment is strictly limited to the defence of these islands, and occurs when the Army Reserve is called out. Any section of tne men, how- ever, may still volunteer for military service abroad, or even for service within the king; dom. The privilege of enlistment into the First Class of the Army Reserve is extended to men who have not served with the Regu- lars, and it will be open to 4,000 of these to belong to a body capable at any time of being drafted for warlike service beyond the seas. Battalions of the Militia may be transferred in bulk, by Orders in Council, to the Army Reserve. Other, portions of the Bill affecting the Regular Army need not be touched here. They shew how thorough has been Mr. 'Hal- dane's study of the complex subject. ALLOTMENTS AND CO-OPERATION. "Back to the land," say the Gozernment, "where every man may be his own allotment holder." How is it to be done? County Coun- cils and other authorities are to be galvanised into activity. They may raise loans to buy land on terms of eighty years' repayment. and compulsory hiring will be possible. Even so, the County Councils may not budge. Small Holdings Commissioners are to ascer- tain from the Board of Agriculture wherever there is a demand for small holdings, and if the County Council declines to provide a scheme the Commissioners 'may do it for them, and charge the cost to the 'County Council. Land may be let to co-operative associations. The county authority may dele- gate its powers to Borou4 and Urban District Councils. For making allotments powers of Rural District Councils are extended to Parish Councils. Where there is no Parish Council the parish meeting may appoint allotment managers. A small holdings committee must be formed by every County C9uncil and see. tion 39 enables a County ' Council to promote the formation of co-operative societies for working holdings: Mr. Lewis Harcourt's measure in practice' is looked forward to with a good deal of interest. OLD-AGE PENSIONS AND INCOME-TAX. Mr. Asquith did better with his Budget this year than last. He promises old-age pen- sions for next year. The Income-Tax still stands at is., but there is an abatement of 3d. on earned incomes where the total is less than £2,000. Employers are now to supply statements of the salaries of their employees. There is an increase of Death Duties on es- tates Over £150,000. The Sinking Fund is strengthened by a special additional contribu- tion of 11 million for the redemption, of debt. OTHER LEGISLATION.

THE YEAR'S OBITUARY.

The year's death-roll includes many distin- guished names. We have space only for the more prominent: The veteran King Oscar 11. of Sweden, Queen Mary of Hanover, the Shah of Persia, Princess Clementine of Saxe-Coburg and Gotha, the Grand Duke of Baden, Colonel George Fitz-George (deldest son of the Duke of Cambridge), the Countess of Cadogan, Marchioness of Ripon, Baroness Adolphe de Rothschild, Mrs. Richard Cad- bury, Sir H. CHRISTMAS SERVICES.

WYCLIFFE CHURCH.

There was a large congregation at Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church on Sunday evening when the choir gave a Christmas Choral Service, consisting of selections from." The Messiah." The interpretation of the music was highly creditable to all concerned, and the service was devotional and inspiring. The choir did justice to the choruses" And the glory,"" For unto us a Child is born," "Behold the Lamb of God," "All we like sheep," and " Hallelujah " which were given with great expression. Miss Marie Houghton, who had come from Bolton after an arduous week's work as the prima donna in the local amateur opera, was in excellent voice and sang "Rejoice greatly" and " I know that my Redeemer livth " with deep feeling. Miss M. E. Knowles and Miss Houghton were heard to distinct advantage in "He shall feed His flock" and " Come unto Me." Miss J. Armstrong sang "0 thou that tellest " and "He was despised and rejected" very effectively, and Miss A. Griffiths gave a beautiful interpretation of "There were shepherds." Mr. A.Griffiths' pure-toned tenor voice found full scope in "Comfort ye" and" Every valley," and Mr. F. Brown sang "Thus saith the Lord," "But who may abide,' "For behold darkness" and "The people that walked in darkness" in a very creditable manner. Valuable service was rendered by an orchestra composed as follows':— First Violins: Mr. E. Clare, and Mr. A. Knowles: ' cello: Mr. H. Bond; bass : Mr. R. Whitfield ; second violins : Mr. W. Brown, Mrs. H. Haviour ; viola: Mr. J. E. Balmford, Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. presided at the organ and Mr. T. F. Wallington conducted. During the evening an appropriate address was delivered by Mr.E. J. Guest, BA.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

Selections from "The Messiah" were given at Bold- street Wesleyan Church where there was a large con- gregation. The soloists were :—Sopranos, Mrs. A. Booth and Miss E. Taylor ; contralto, Miss Lily Fair- hurst; tenor' Mr. J. Clayton; basses, Mr. W. Warburton and Mr. H. Plumpton, each of whom sang with taste and expression. The chorus acquitted themselves splendidly, the " Hallelujah Chorus" being given most expressively. Mr. Frank Hickman was at the organ. An address was delivered by the Rev. W. Mellor, of Padgate,

===== 0002601_19071228_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN CHURCH.

A Christmas choral service was held at this church the choir giving selections from" The Messiah." Mr. T. W. Pearce presided at the organ, and played the overture and the Pastoral Symphony. The choir was heard to great advantage in the choruses "And the glory of the Lord," "All we like sheep," " Hallelujah" and "Worthy is the Lamb." Mr. W. Tynan sang "Comfort ye" and "Every Valley " ; Miss Hipkiss "There were Shepherds," "Rejoice greatly " and "Come unto Him " ; Mrs. J. E. Hannan," Then shall the eyes," •` He shall feed His flock," "He was despised " ; Mr. E. H. Pearce," Why do the nations" and" The trumpet shall sound" (with trumpet obligato by Mr. W. Rutter), all acquitting themselves admirably. The Rev. E. J. Gawne gave an address.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. PAUL'S CHURCH.

At 'St. Paul's Church on Sunday evening a number of Christmas carols were sung by the choir in the presence of a large congregation. The Vicar conducted a shortenedsery ice assisted by the Rev.E. E. Peters,curate. Mr. E. Wilkes Presided at the organ, and the carols, which were very sweetly sung, were "Sleep, Holy Rabe," " There dwelt in old Judea" and" Ring out ye bells." Mr. Pownall gave a very able rendering of " For behold darkness" and "The people that walked in darkness," and the other soloists were Mr. Walker and Miss Armstrong.

CHRISTMAS DAY MATCHES.

G. T. P. G. T. P Warrington Bradford 1 3 11 Leeds 1 2 8 1 Wigan 5 9 37 Batley Wakefield Oldham Rochdale Hull K. R 2 2 10 Keighley Hunslet Runcorn 2 5 19 St. Helens Broughton R. l4 14 Salford .l 210 1 Hull , 3 2 12 York

Warrington's Fine Play at Leigh. Warrington took a strong team to Leigh on Wednes- day, Brooks replacing Lees, and Landor claming in as right wing. Leigh were short of Bennett and T. John- son. Fine, dry weather prevailed and Warrington ! excelled in the good going to such an extent that their combination quite outclassed Leigh and reversed tha t verdict of the Lancashire Cup Tie on the same ground.! Wednesday's match counted in three Leagues, the Northern, the Lancashire, and the South-West Lanca- shire, two points falling to the winners in each. There! were 4,000 spectators. Teams : Warrington: Tilley, full back; Fish, Dickenson,.! Taylor and Landor threequarters ; Brooks and Jenkins ; half backs ; Thomas,A. Boardman, P. Boardman,Naylor, Harmer and Shugars forwards. Leigh: Stout, full back; S. Johnson, Clarkstm, Win- stantley and Neville, threequarters ; Molyneux and ' Whittaker, half backs; O'Shauglinessy, Smith, Gallop, 1 Ashton, Webster and Walsh forwards. Referee: Mr. E. H. Smirk Preston.

Leigh lost the toss and started against the wind-and had all the best of the first exchanges. The.Warringtoe half-backs at last showed their ability Jenkins dribbling cleverly and just being stopped on the line by Stout. Leigh broke away from the ensuing scrum and Smith being obstructed a penalty was given against Warring-- ton. From the resulting kick which was accepted by Dickenson at midfield the last named player kicked instead of passing to Fish. Fish followed up and Stout mulling Fish apparently scored a fair try, but Referee Smirk decided he was off-side and brought the ball. back to half-way. Fish neatly cross-kicked and Gallop made a good mark at the critical moment. Dickenson took the ball from the kick and threw wide to Jenkins who darted through. Brookes joining in dodged several of the Leigh forwards and passing to Taylor the latter went over instead of passing to Landor, scoring a well worked for try.. Thomas- kicked a goal from a difficult angle the ball just going over the bar. This was almost Warrington's first visit to Leigh quarters although they were playing with the wind. Molyneux at this stage was playinga remarkably good waiting game his catching and kicking being very fine although it certainly was not the game to kick so much against the wind. Leigh apparently were well' pleased that Warrington could only get five points with a gale at their backs. Half-time: Warrington,. 5, ; points; Leigh, nil. Warrington kicked off and S. Johnson receiving oni the wing put in a short run and kick which Fish took 1 high up at the second attempt, the bouncing of the ball I it Fish's hands beating S. Johnson. Fish put in ai! characteristic run half the length of the field, scoring. a magnificent try by beating several of the Leigh backs.! and finally going on the outside of Stout. Thomas's, kick at gaal was not successful. Gallop kicked Off but f only sent the hall to Naylor standing close up and be , putting in a dribble in which all the visiting forwards joined took the ball to the home line. Warrington forwards were at this stage completely out-playing the ; Leigh men. From a lofty kick and follow up Brookes arrived at the same time as Stout and took the ball on the bounce and scored a brilliant try for Thomas to kick a goal. Warrington after the kick I off immediately returned to -the attack and Jenkins"' final pass being forward spoiled an apparently easy 1 chance of scoring by Thomas. Leigh now paid- their ; first visit to the Warrington quarters by means of long kicks. Their passing from the scrimmage was however I slow and the final pass to Neville could not be of any I use. Thomas relieved to half-way and Molyneux pet in a clever kick, Neville follow* up to find that Tilley was just in time to kick into touch in goal.. Leigh obtained the ball from the five yards stiTtUll and Molyneux cross-kicking sent the ball over the Warring- ton line for Fish to make dead. The visitors went away again from the drop out and Taylor taking up the running took the ball to the line but just tell short. On playing the ball Jenkins managed to secure and score behind the posts for-Thomas to kick a goal. Leigh at this stage was entirely outplayed. Dickenson doubled through to Leigh centres and passing over S. Johnson's head at the right moment enabled Fish to score another try, Thomas goaling. Gallop again restarted with a very weak kick and Stingers received and dribbled finely, Jenkins and Thomas taking up the running, ending by Jenkins scoring a try and outwitting Johnson in the dribble. rbomas again converted. Final : Warrington, 4 goals, 6 tries (26 points) ; Leigh nil.

'THE EXAMINER.

DECEMBER 2Eth 1907.

The Parliamentary Corn-

TRADE mittee of the Trades Union UNIONISTS AND Congress anticipates the OVERTIME. worst winter, in respect of unemployment,that we have had for some years past, and its views on this subject are possibly well founded. As we have frequently had occasion to point out, and as experience invariably proves, periods of good trade are inevitably followed by periods ot depression of longer or shorter duration, just as the anti-cyclone follows the cyclone. Prosperity causes a rise of wages, and the resulting dearness of commodities is followed by a slackening of demand. These are the truisms ; and yet they are far from being com- monly understood. The Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress evidently believes that the evil day can be staved off by a more general distribution of the work that will remain to be done when the expected depression has actually set in. It has issued a circular, the object of which is to impress on the members of trade unions the necessity of alleviating the pressure of bad times by refusing to work overtime. Overtime says the circular, "is brutal, having regard to the nuniber of men unemployed while it exists." Trade unionists are ask to co-operate to abolish systematic overtime, or to restrict it to the narrowest possible limits, and penalise it to the fullest possible extent.

Anyone who has given attention to the subject must be inclined to sympathise with the terms of the circular,but will at the same time appreciate the great difficulty there must be in giving practical application to them. Systematic overtime is, undoubtedly, brutalising, as were the long hours that were common in almost all employments fifty or sixty years ago. It is not merely that it leaves no leisure for recreation or for intellectual enjoy- ment. The worst effect probably is that money earned by working overtime is pretty commonly regarded as if it were money won on a race, or "money found." It is an adventitious addition to the week's wage, and is very liable to be wasted in ways which add to thw evil effects of the long hours of labour. If overtim e could be abolished altogether the result would be beneficial, there is little doubt,:both to the health and to the morals of the community

llf there were nothing to be considered but the I best conditions of labour, the moral and physical health of the working man, the raising of the standard o: life among the working-classes; and if, as is suggested by the circular of the Parlia- mentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress, work which is not done by one man in extra hours of labour could always be given to another man who would otherwise be without employ- ment, the abolition of overtime would be one of the greatest of public blessings. Even this circular suggests, however, that abolition is not in all cases practicable. The pro- posal is that it should be either strictly limited or more heavily penalised. The penalty is already considerable. Payment for overtime is usually made at the rate of time and a half. In most instances it is probable that the employer is recouped for his extra payment in wages by the fact that he is able to keep down his establish- ment charges, and to do with a comparatively small equipment of machinery what would require a much larger one if his works ran for no more than the regulation day. Whether the present penalty could be increased without bringing about a stoppage of orders in many, and perhaps in most, instances is at least doubtful. But in works in which overtime is systematic—that is, where it goes on from one year's end to another almost without intermission—there is a clear case for the exercise of such pressure as the trade-union circular suggests. An employer whose works are kept, going every day for a much longer time than is now considered to be a fair day's labour is plainly in a position to extend them and to give employment to a larger number of hands, probably without any actual increase in the wages bill. On the other hand, however, there must be many instances in which the overtime is spasm3dic and necessary. Orders are received on the understanding that they must be completed within a certain specified time. They could not be taken at all if it were necessary to lay down new plant for their completion: In such cases as these it does not appear that interference of any sort would be judicious; for it cannot be assumed that if the contract could not be executed in the specified time by one set of employers it could by another. The order might be of such magnitude that it could not be anywhere carried out within the stipulated period without making an addition to the regular hours of labour. Tne trade unionists to whom the circular is addressed should, therefore, proceed with caution and discrimination, or they may produce effects which they did not anticipate, and which may be gravely injurious to their own interests.

We are thankful that we A NARROW have not had to record any ESCAPE. tragic disaster locally during this Christmas week. It has often been remarked that some of the worst catastrophies of the year happen at Christmas time and there was certainly a narrow escape on Monday when the smash between a passenger train and a goods train occurred on the Cheshire Lines railway near Sankey. The fog was so dense at the time that anything might have happened, but luckily and miraculously no one was killed, the most serious damage being to rolling stock, merchandise and the permanent way. Traffic was suspended for about seven hours, and as a consequence of this some curious things happened which will cause many people to vividly but not pleasantly recall the incident in after years. Tee accident happened about 5.20 p.m- and com- pletely blocked the main line between Liv erpool and Manchester. For some reason or other the officials at Liverpool continued to dispatch the hourly expresses as though nothing had happened with the result that these trains were held up before they had gone many miles out of Liverpool. The passengers spent a weary time in durance which was particularly vile,and extending in many instances five or six hours. Dozens of regular train- goers had had no food since mid-day, there was none on board the trains, and the dense fog out- side discouraged attempts at foraging in an unknown country. Why the passengers were not informed of the accident at Liverpool, and why the trains were dispatched into the foggy unknown, aTe two questions which will be put in furious terms to the Cheshire Lines Committee. Railway passengers always have a grievance, but in this case their sore com- plaints are legitimate. Bing half-starved to death is apt to make people oblivious of the spirit of thankfulness which would otherwise have animated them at so lucky an escape from fatal disaster.

Sir Oliver Lodge in his PEMMICAN report to the British Asso- KNOWLEDGE. ciation on Secondary Schools

condemns the idea that an abstract of all knowledge should be stuffed into the brains of the young tather than that the foundations of ability to learn should be more truly laid by a general system of instruction comprising what used to be known mainly as" the three R's." On the Continent, especially in Germanv, children before the age of twelve are kept to simple sub- jects' which are so drilled into them that they are not forgotten in later life. What is known as "Pemmican " is, literally, dried and pounded meat, prepared with fat and raisins in a con- centrated form, for long journeys and voyages, as in the Arctic regions. To say that this sort of food—useful under exceptional circumstances—is generally desirable in everyday life is to stato something contrary to fact. There is enough dyspepsia about already without adding to it. Nevertheless, although human beings instinctively reject unsuitable food for their bodies, when it corns to education they are full of fads. it is quite time that men like Sir Oliver Lodge pointed out that pounded and compressed knowledge, prepared with fallacies and tit-bits in a concen- trated form, is most unsuitable for the long voyage of life, especially when the necessaries which serve as ammunition in the contests of intellect depend ever more and more on solid foundations if they are to be really serviceable iuteruationally.

Lord Kelvin's remains have THE LATE been buried in Westminster LORD KELVIN, Abbey. Now that the dirge music has rolled away and the pall has been laid aside, we may recall some of those additions to the sum total of know- ledge for which the names in the Scientists' Corner may be said to stand. Sir Isaac Newton was born in 1642, so that the range, in respect of time, is considerable. Beside his grave that of Lord Kelvin was digged. It was in the year of the Great Fire of Intidon, 1666, that Newton found the prismatic colours and analysed the sun's light, and his construction of the first reflecting telescope belongs to two years later. The year, by the bye, on which we are enter- ing sees the tercentenary of the discovery of various: telescopes and other instruments of astronomy. Charts of the moon were forthcoming about 1670, and Greenwic4 Observatory was founded in 1675. The great yeas of that century w when Newton's Principia was published, containing his theories of gravi t ation, the refraction of light, and so on, and the whole system, indeed, as now taught was demonstrated. Herschel— whose funeral, as well as Darwin's, Lnrd Kelvin attended—lies not many yards away. His discovery of Uranus and its satellites—he really discovered more than there were—belongs to 1781; his construction of a 40t. focal length telescope to 1789. He discovered three volcanic mountains in the moon, and gave the Royal Society, in 1802, a catalogue of 5,000 nebula and clusters of stars. His 20ft. focal length telescope he tcok t the Cape, and with it made his observations there. Evolution, with which Darwin's name is asaccir.ted, Eastern

Lord Kelvin has been a pioneer in the electrical discovery and applica- tion of the nineteenth century. His record rounds off the scientific triumph in many realms. Edison has called him the greatest among his contem- poraries, and it is right to recall that long ago Lord Kelvin gave us that theory of electric oscillations which, practically elaberated, resulted in wireless telegraphy.

* * * There were numbers of people at the match between Warrington and the All Blacks on Saturday whom one does not often see or expect to see at football matches. Imperialists and Little Englanders alike all found the fixture an attractive one, and all rejoiced in the creditable victory achieved by our local lads over the muscular men from" down under." * The Member for Warrington is one of the three gentlemen who have been presented with a framed portrait of the Warrington team which won the Northern Union Cup last April. We hope it will find a place on the walls of Mr. Crosfield's new home at Hyde Park Gate. The other recipients of portraits are Sir Gilbert Greenall, and the President of the Club, Mr. J. E. Warren.

* * * Robert Blatchford, the Socialist, says that ii he could be sure that no child should be hungry, no woman should be ill-used, and poverty should dis- appear he would give every shilling he owned, and his life as well, That offer, in billiard phrase, (says " London Opinion ") is a dead safe miss under the cash. Nevertheless, Blatchfords' reputation for sincerity will not suffer. * * * It was indeed pleasing to see the Parr Hall crowded it for the Warrington Musical S iety's performance of "The Messiah on Boxing Da The Society made a new departure in producing e great oratorio as a Bank Holiday attraction and the success achieved was such unmistakable evidence of public appreciation that we may venture to hope for an annual repetition. "The Messiah" is inseparably associated with the Christmas season, and often as we bear the work it never seems to lose any of its charm. * * * Warrington still holds its own at the musical festivals and there were more gratifying local successes at Chester on Boxing Day. The Apollo Prize Male Choir won the male choirs' competition unopposed, which sounds easy to do, but the adjudicator paid them the tribute of saying that their interpretations were excellent and fully deserved the prize. They are therefore entitled to take full credit for the victory. Miss Lily Fairhurst and the Lyric Quartette Party also added to their laurels. * * * The local soap libel case has been amicably settled. The " Daily Mail" and the ' " Daily Mirror" have withdrawn all statements concerning Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., w hich " might be understood as conveying imputations upon their personal honour and integrity." No information is given as to the terms of settlement, but it is reported that they are "entirely satisfactory " from the Warrington firm's point of view. The first claim against the associated newspapers which libelled the parties to the soap combine seems to have been an excellent " Lever for helping others to obtain satisfaction. * * * It was Christmas-eve, and at the foot of the bed a pair of tiny stockings and a" pilrow-slip "had been bung up. But little Molly, aged five, was restless, and found no sleep. At last she spoke of her trouble in a roundabout•way. "Daddy told me to hang up the pillow-slip, didn't he ?" 4 Yes, dear." "Will Santa Claus know Daddy told me ?" "Yes, dear. Why do you ask ? " "I fought praps Santa Claus wouldn't leave me anyfink if he saw the pillow- slip and didn't know !" And presently a relieved and sleep-laden voice added softly : "If Santa Claus knows that Daddy told me to hang it up,' he'll know that it isn't me that's greedy." * * * In the midst of life we are in debt. —illizaicr. A green Christmas seems to be the order of the years, though twelve months ago there was a startling last minute change from grePn to white. That sudden heavy snowfall will not readily be forgotten by those people who were out for the evening! The two days prior to Christmas this year were remarkable for dense fog, but the festal day broke bright and clear and proved in all atmospheric respects very enjoy- able. The weather has since become steadily colder and more seasonable, and the first fall of snow does not appear to be far off. Employment seems to have been very good of late, and they who had no Christmas cheer must indeed have been in sad case. We hope that still there are good times coming. * * * During the brief period of Christmas pressure at the Warrington Post Office upwards of two million missives were dealt with. Notwithstanding the special arrangements and the fervent requests, there was very little posting in advance by the general public. We cannot say that we are surprised. It is human nature to procrastinate, and then, too, many people have not got their cards and gifts ready or even purchased several days before Christmas. Something like 20,000 missives were posted in advance but as compared with 2,000,000 they were but a drop in the ocean of seasonable greetings. It has been said more than once in times past that the custom of sending Christmas cards was on the decline, but the statement is not borne out by the facts. The only thing there has been a reduction in this season, is in the number of boxes of cigars and similar gifts from business people to customers, buyers and the like. The new Corrupt Practices Act supplies the reason. * * * A post-Christmas reflection —Does the gradual supersession of the home-made plum pudding by the shop-made variety bode ill for the continuance of the traditional Christmas dinner ? Will the ruthless invasion of professionalism, as exemplified by the ready-made plum pudding ordered from one of the big confectionery establishments, ruin the Christmas dinner as it is said to have ruined sport? These are questions which ought not to be shirked by those who desire to see the traditional Christmas perpetuated. The trade in ready-made puddings (says the "Liverpool Post and Mercury ") has now grown to enormous pro- portions, and this year the increase is reported to be even more remarkable than usual. If the figures could be stated with any degree of exactness it would probably strike terror into the hearts of lovers of the real old-fashioned Christmas to learn how many of the puddings which were served up on the tables of Liverpool suburbs on Christmas Day were delivered at the door as finished products requiring only the final boiling. It is a significant fact that it is in the homes of the newly-married couples that the pro- fessional pudding is mainly making headway.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

DROPPED DOWN DEAD.

- ! Edward Tickle, residing at Lacy-street, Widnes, dropped down dead while working at Messrs. Bolton's copper works on Monday morning. He was employed as a ladler, and was thirty-eight years of age.

RUNCORN BLACKLISTER COMMITTED. James Robinson, a Runcorn blacklister, was sentenced to a month's hard labour at Runcorn on Monday on a charge of being druuk and disorderly on Saturday night, RUNCORN WIDOW'S NEGLECT.—RELIEVING OFFICER AS JUDGE.

I At Runcorn on Monday, Annie Wakefield, a widow, , of Millstone-yard. was summoned for neglecting her children. Inspector Rogers, National Society 'for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, said the woman and four children—a boy, seventeen, and a girl fifteen, and and two younger boys—lived in a two-roomed cottage, and slept in one bed. The house was dirty and filthy, and, as there was no through ventilation, the atmosphere was putrid. The defendant's husband had been ill for some months, and died in November. The woman refused to go into the Workhouse with her children. The children were verminous and dirty and badly clothed. The relieving officer refused to give her out-relief because of her character. It was necessary to remove the children, for their moral and physical welfare. The defendant did not get relief because she was not a sober woman. I The Magistrates Clerk (Mr. Johnson) : Does the relieving officer take that upon himself, without bring- ing it before the guardians. The Irspector : Yes; he is perfectly within his right to do so. The Chairman (Mr. Thomas) : The relieving officer is a teetotaler, is he not? The Inspector: I believe he is, your worship. The Chairman: If he saw her take one glass of beer, that would be enough? The Inspector : I don't know. The Chairman: I think it is rather hard lines. At any rate, he might have taken the case before the guardians. It is an exceptional case. The Inspector: It is not always done. The Chairman : It ought to be done. Dr. M`Donnell described the condition of the chil- dren and the house, which he said was not fit for fa family. The defendant said if she had another chance she would do her best for the children. The bench adjourned the case for six weeks, and told her to apply for relief. The Chairman said he thought she was entitled to relief. If she had ss. to the 10s. earned by her eldest son the woman might manage.

ST. HELENS.

A ST. HELENS GIRL SENT TO A REFORMATORY.

Margaret Rigby, aged fifteen, a respectable-looking girl, living with her parents in Oxford-street, was charged at St. Helens on Monday with stealing a jacket, value 15s. On November 29th Ada Else, of Hardshaw-street,who attended Moorfiat Schools, missed her jacket from the cloakroom. Detective Curran arrested the girl Rigby, and she admitted ;taking the jacket, cutting the buttons off, and selling it for a shilling to a woman in Lowe-street. The Chief Con- stable said prisoner's parents were very respectable. The girl's father complained that she had got beyond his control. She went away to Liverpool and other places, and kept late hours. She had stolen money and other things,and in the interest of the girllherself and everyone else concerned he thought she ought to be eat away. The bench committed the girl to a reforma- sory for four years. MAGISTRATES AND POLICE. ANGRY CHIEF CONSTABLE.

At St. Helens, on Monday, Thomas Clayton, licensee of the Feathers Hotel, Westfield-street, was summoned on three informations for being drunk on his licensed premises, for supplying drink to drunken persons, and for permitting drunkenness. Mr. Rigby Swift (Messrs. Swift and Garner) defended. Sergeant GlPave, examined by the Chief Constable, said that on Saturday night, the 14th inst., he visited tha Feathers Inn. As he entered he saw Clayton stagger- ing into the bar. He was drunk, sad witness told him this. Clayton slbid, "All right, I will go with you." Witness told him to get his jacket, and Clayton went upstairs for it. Defendant afterwards began to abuse witness, waving his arms about,and he then arrested him. Witness had to force his way out of the house. Some- one struck him in the ribs, and outside he had to arrest one of the crowd for violence. At the palice office Dr. Davies put Clayton through several tests. He failed to pronounce " contour " and when he was set to walk along a line he staggered and completely failed, and he also failed to write his name aed address in a legible manner. The Chief Inspector said to him " You are drunk." Clayton said "Yes, I admit it, but I object to being brought here." Later on when he saw the Chief Constable Clayton abused and made charges against the police. He was undoubtedly drunk. In cross-examination witness admitted that there had been some trouble during the afternoon at this house. Police Constables Couts and Swift corroborated, the latter stating that when he got to the police office Clayton asked him if they had a pair of boxing gloves, and he would put one or two of them through it. Mr. Swift asked one of the officers to repeat his evidence. The Chief Constable said he did uot think this was fair. Mr. Swift said it was perfectly fair, and from the way in which Police Constable Couts repeated his evidence he was going to ask the Bench to say that it was physically and mentally impossible for a man to repeat the story in the way he did unless he had studied it very closely. The Chief Constable said he objected to the innuendo that the St. Helens police were coached in their evidence. Police Constable Whalley and Chief Inspector Goodall described what took place at the police office.

POLICE DOCTOR UPSETS THE CASE. Dr. Davies was called by the police, and described what took place. He found that Clayton was not sober, but he knew exactly what he was doing. Mr. Swift: Are you prepared to swear to this bench that he was drunk? Witness: No. The Chief Constable : Are you prepared to swear that he was sober? Witness: He was under the influence of drink, but I take it a man is drunk when he cannot look after himself, or is muddled, and cannot give a reasonable answer. He was quite clear as to what he was doing. The Chief Constable proceeded to further examine the doctor.

Mr. Swift objected to this. The Chief Constable: I want to ask him if it is not true that he has given me two certificates, and they both vary. The Clerk: You are not entitled to ask that: Chief Constable Ellerington gave evidence, and said that because of Clayton's excited condition he locked him up. Sergeant Small corroborated. Mr. Swift emphatically asked the bench to say that there was no case on which it would be necessary to call witneSses for the defence.lf a man was taken to the police office and kept there for an hour or more they could get any number of policemen to say that he was drunk, but the independent witness, Dr. Davies, had said that he was not guilty of the charge the police brought. The magistrates dismissed the charge of being drunk. Clayton was then charged with supplying drink to two drunken men and with permitting drunkenness. Police- constable Griffiths and Smith spoke to seeing two men I named Flynn and Gordon. who had been turned out of the Liverpool Arms, go into the Feathers, where they were served. They spoke to the barmaid, and she said, "I did not think they were so drunk. I had my back to them when they came in." Mr. Swift said there was no dispute that the two men were very drunk. Several witnesses were called. Mr. Swift. addressed the court for the defence, and said the bench would not have heard anything of these two charges if there had not been later on that day trouble between Clayton and the police. 1 The Chief Constable said that statement was grossly unfair. ' Mr. Swift: The bench will draw their own con- clusions as to how a TRUMPERY CASE LIKE THIS is brought into court. , The magistrates, after retirement, returned into I court, when the Chairman (Mr. W. J. Thomson) said 1 the cases would be dismissed. They agreed that the ' barmaid served the men,but her attention ought to have I been drawn to the condition of the men, and they ought ; not to have been allowed to go into the place. They ought to have been arrested and locked up. The Chief Constable said he would not allow that to go out. He must have the constable back into the box to show that he had no opportunity of arresting them. Mr. Glover, M.P. : Here is one of the policemen in the witness-box tells us that these men were ejected from a public-house. They allow them to be ejected, and follow them up drunk and let them go into a public house and be served. That is our position; it is unfair. The Chief Constable said that if the men had gone home when they were ejected from the Liverpool Arms nothing would have been done. He would not stand things like that. Mr. Glover thereupon got up and left the Bench. Police-Constable Griffiths, in answer to the Chief Constable, said that when these two men were turned out of the Liverpool Arms he was 168 yards away, and he was following them np to warn the landlord of the 1 Feathers bnt they were served before he got there. The Chief Constable: 1 think it is most unfair ; it is scandalous. It is out of all reason to say that the policeman should have prevented this when he was 1 following them up for the purpose of warning them. Mr. Thomson: You have made 7our protest. The Chief Constable: Yes, but it is monstrous.

It was with extreme regret the friends of the late , Mrs. Harriet Mellor heard of her death, which took place at the residence of her son, Mr. W. A. Mellor, Knutsford-road, on Monday afternoon, at the advanced age of 73 years. The deceased lady had been in indifferent health for the past month or two, hut had , only been confined to her bed from the Monday prior to her death. The end came very quietly and peacefully. Although never having taken an active part in the public life of the town, having always confined her-' self to what she considered to be the sacred duties of hearth and home, Mrs. Mellor was well known to the older Warringtonians as the widow of the late, Mr. Isaac Mellor, of Horsemarket-street, a gentleman who won for himself the esteem and affection of all. The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery, on Thursday, Rev. T. Miller officiating at the house and graveside. The principal mourners were :- •Mrs.A. Pitt (daughter), Mr. and Mrs. W. A. Mellor (son and daughter-in-law), Mr. I. Mellor (son), Mrs. E. Ford, and Mr. H. T. Ford.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

FORMER NEWTONIAN'S SUICIDE.

Last week an inquest was held at Bury on Alexander Davidson, aged 44 years, of Heaton Fold, Bury, who died at Bury Infirmany. Deceased was formerly a resident in Mercer-street, Newton-le-Willows, and was a beater in connection with paper-making at the Newton le-Willows mill of Messrs. Gillespie and Alason,Limited but just before last Christmas was thrown out of work in consequence of the closing of the mill. After a time Mr. Davidson obtained employment at Bury, to which town he and his family removed. It appears that since then matters had not gone on very well, and some ten weeks ago he was thrown out of employment. He had since been unable to get work,and on Monday after- noon he locked the bedroom door and cut his throat. A verdict of" Febo de se " was returned. DRAWING-ROOM MEETING.

By the kindness of Mr. and Mrs. Ai, ey, a most successful drawing-room meeting was held last Friday at Sandfield Hall. Newton-le-Willows, in aid of the diocesan branch of the Waifs and Strays Society.—The Rector of Lowton (the Rev. R. Smith) presided, and the Society's work was explained by Mr. G. D. Killey chairman of the branch, and the Rev. R. Hawley Sharpe, diocesan organizing secretary. Despite the unfavourable weather a good number of friends assembled. The meeting resulted in the addition of £l2 6s. to the funds of the society. The speakers pointed out that this work was compelled to go on extending its scope, for the needs of the children were imperative. In particular the speakers pointed to the local branch of the Silver Thimble League, which holds an annual sale of work contributed by the members, and of which Miss Watkins is the secretary. HAYDOCK URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held last Thursday, Mr. J. Robinson presiding. The Clerk read correspondence with the Board of Trade, and also sub- mitted copy of draft regulations and by-laws proposed to be made for the working of the St. Helens Corpora- tion Tramways, the Board having asked for the Council's observations thereon, in reply to which the Clerk, after pointing out clerical errors, called atten- tion to the fact that the recommendation of Colonel von Donop in his report as to special care being taken when passing the Haydock National and Haydock Co'liery British Schools: was not mentioned in the regulations. Resolved that the Clerk's action be approved. The Clerk reported he had appeared before the Assessment Committee of the Warrington Union, and objected to this assessment, and that same had been referred to the Union, Valuer.—At a meeting of the Gas and Water Committee the Clerk submitted a report on the fire appliances, and after consideration thereon, he was requested to prepare a report and scheme for the organising of a fire brigade to be formed of workmen of the Council and Messrs. R. Evans and Company, Ltd. Prior to the members separating the Chairman wished them a Merry Christmas and a Happy New Year, sentiments which were reciprocated.

Presentation to Dr. Hodgkinson. There was a pleasing ceremony at a meeting of the Board of Management of the Warrington Infirmary held yesterday afternoon. The Rev. H. V. Pigot presided, and there were also present :—Revs. C. Harvey Cook, John Yonge, and Father J. P. Whittle, Dr. E. E. Bowden, Messrs. W. Daintith, John Fair dough, W. J. Forshaw, C. Jackson Holmes, J. Charlton Parr, Roger C. Parr, W. Peter Rylands, J. H. Smethurst, George Walker, C. M. Watkins, C. R. Brown (senior house surgeon), and J. H. J. Hampson (secretary).—The Chairman presented Dr. A. Hodgkinson, the late senior house surgeon, with a purse of gold subscribed for by the members of the Board as a token of esteem on the occasion of his marriage. He expressed regret that the institution was losing the services of Dr. Hodgkinson, who had been a very faithful aud valuable servant, but he was glad to learn that the doctor was going to settle down in Watrington. On behalf of the Board he wished him every happiness and success. (Applause.) Dr. Hodgkinson feelingly responded. JORDAN ALMONDS Is. 6d. per lb. at ASHLEY'S.—Advt.
