Local Successes at Chester Eisteddfod.

Warrington competitors were again to the fore at the 19th Christmas Eisteddfod held in the Music Hall, Chester, on Boxing Day and some very gratifying comments were pessed upon their singing by the adjudicator (Mr. Harry Evans, Liverpool). In the choral contest for male voices (30 to 50 voices), Birkenhead Welsh Male Voice Choir failed to put in an appearance so that Warrington Apollo (conducted by Mr. Harry Berrey) were the only competitors. The test pieces were " Where is He ?' (Beethoven) and "Myfanwy " (Harry Ennis). In awarding the prize of £l5 and a silver mounted baton for conductor to the Apollo Choir, the adjudicator remarked upon the excellence of tone and attack shown in their interpre- tation of '1 Where is He ?" Every word, be said came out distinctly and it was an excellent perform- ance throughout. He would like to hear a choir which could excel it. With the rendering of the part song "Myfanwy," he had no fault to find except that the tempo was a little bit too quick. While he would have liked a contest for that event, there was no mistake that the choir fully deserved the prize. There were four competitors for the contralto solo " Lascia Chio Pianga " (Handel), and the adjudicator said he had no hesitation in awarding the first prize of £1 to Miss Lily Fairhurst who was a thorough artiste and sang very soulfully a piece which was a most difficult one for amateurs to attempt. The relinquishing of tone in the piano passages was excellent and her full rich voice was heard to advantage in the louder parts.—ln the mixed quartette contest there were three competing parties Lyric Quartette, Warrington' Deva Quartette Chester, and Brythoniad Quartette, Wrexham. The test piece was " Yea, though I walk " (Sullivan). The adjudicator said there was no difficulty in finding the winner. The singing of the Lyric Quartette reached a very high level—in fact it was a rare thing to meet such a combination, even in professionalism. They sang with beautiful and excellent tone and the piece was charming to listen to, being most artistically interpreted. The . " tit bit" of the Eisteddfod was the singing of the Lyr:c Quartette and he awarded them the prize of £2. 'LINSEED COMPOUND' for Coughs, Colds, and Bronchial troubles. 91d. & 1/li. THE VOLUNTEERS. DISTRIBUTION OF CHRISTMAS PRIZES.

Monday evening witnessed another prize distribution in connection with the Ist Volunteer Battalion (P.W.V.) South Lancashire Regiment, the prizes on this occasion being the annual Christmas .gifts of the various tradespeople and manufacturers In the town. The proceedings took place at the Drill Hall, and there was a large muster. The officers present were Lieutenant-Colonel Edelsten (commandi ,g officer), Major J. D. Fairclough, Captains R. Smith, P.N. Avery, H. Owen; Lieutenants R. M. Roberts and Max Woods and Captain and Adjutant Mott. . Lieutenant-Colonel EDELSTEN began his speech by expressing the great indebtedness of the Corporation to the donors of the two hundred prizes which showed that their Volunteering was appreciated. Continuing he said that possibly people would have wondered whether his remarks in praise of the battaliun the previous week were justified. That morning he had received the official report of the officer immediately over them and it was very satisfactory. It read -- "An efficent and well-commanded unit. Complete in all respects." (Applause.) That report went before the General, Si- Charles Burnett. who added the marginal note, "I agrae. An excellent battalion." That was dated the sth of this month, so that Sir Charles wrote that before he saw them last week on what he (Colonel Edelsten) presumed, Sir Charles had seen in camp, and from what he had heard of them in other ways. It was pleasing to think that they had reached a standard at the end of their time under the old Volunteering system. Alluding to the territorial army scheme, the speaker said there was notbing in the new scheme to interfere with a man earning his daily bread. If. sufficient Volunteers were not forthcoming, it would not be because the terms of service were too hard, but because patriotism in the country was dead. They required one man out of every 100 to volunteer. If they could get that they would get 300,000 men in the Territorial Army, and it was considered that that would be sufficient to secure England in its present position. That was the very minimum. If they did not get the 300,000 men some more drastic measures would have to be taken. Things could not go on as before. The old state of things had not been good enough. Why— Because things had been changed elsewhere. The Territorial Army was to protect these shores. If we could not get the requisite number by voluntary means, we must have enforced service. There were 2,000 men in Warrington who ought to join the Volunteers. If they got an enforced service those fellows would have a very much worse time of it than ff they had joined the Volunteers—infinitely worse. Therefore, it was to the interest of all of them, as well as their duty, that the scheme to which he had alluded should succeed. Employers of labour were recognizing that fact, and he was convinced it was their intention, at least of most of them,to help the Territorial Force. Having apologised for the absence of Mrs. Edlesten, who had promised to distribute the prizes, the Colonel concluded by wishing all the men a happy Christmas and a prosperous New Year. (Applause.) Colonel Edelsten then distributed the prizes.

NEWTON MYSTERY SOLVED. A few days ago Mrs. Ellen Worrall, aged thirty-three years, of 23, Alexander-street, Newton, was reported by her family as missing. On Wednesday her body was found in a ditch behind the Ben Johnson Hotel, Worsley Mesnes. Upon the body being examined a cord was found attached to the neck. Mrs. Worrall had been in a depressed state for some few weeks. There is no uspicion of foul play.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

The New Zealanders' share of the gate on Saturday amounted to £260 10s. 11d., Warrington receiving

There is no question of the fact that the All Blacks are playing fine football in their big matches. They only succumbed to last season's Cup Finalists by a single point at both Oldham and Warrington, and on Boxing Day they surprised Hunslet by running them to a draw of eleven points. The All Blacks ought to have won this match and been the first to lower the Hunslet colours; for they were nine points ahead at half-time, but four goals from the foot of Albert Goldthorpe altered matters in the second half. Messenger placed another magnificent goal from half- way. The keenness of the struggle resulted in a little temper being shown and one of the home forwards received marching orders in the second half.

Warrington have also been busy since Saturday and the harvest of 12 league points in two days is about a record. The points have to be divided among three leagues. In the way of scoring too, the Cup holders put up some good figures. On Christmas Day they trounced Leigh at Mather-lane by 26 points to nil, and the following day defeated Widnes at War- rington by 20 pints to three. These figures are eloquent of the team's scoring power.

The match at Leigh was one of the finest seen there for some time, and the way the Warrington men ran round Leigh fairly staggered the " locals," who fancied they could repeat the result of the Lancashire Cup tie. Even then Warrington were far the superior side, but things failed to "come off." On Wednes- day there was no hitch in the proceedings from War- rington's point of view. Every man was in form, and four of the six tries came from movements which commenced at half-way. Dickenson played a great game, and to-day he is certainly show- ing the finest football of his career. Not long ago a weak knee seemed likely to terminate his football prematurely, but since it has got strong the lengthy "George "has fairly distinguished himself at centre. The way he feeds the left wing is capital, and the change from the right seems to have suited him. Brooks and Jenkins were a very strong pair at Leigh, the little Welshman playing a dazzling game and twice beating the defence. Fish was also very active, and the way he replied to Sam Johnson's kick with a try from half-way reminded one of old times.

The one surprising thing about the Boxing Day match with Widnes was the display of the visitors. They played capital football, and had far more of the game than the score 20-3 indicates. Why they are at the bottom of two Leagues is a mystery. Both forwards and backs seemed smart, and it was only lack of finishing power which prevented the " Chemicals " running Warrington closer. Lally played a fine game at half. What Widnes want is a Dickenson or Hogg behind him! Warrington seemed to be feeling the effects of the hard game at Leigh and their efforts wera rather spasmodic. Jenkins was again in the thick of the fray, but he received poor support from Lees, who was distinctly slow and unenterprising. Brooks went back to the wing position and quite outshone Fish with a couple of tries and some brilliant running and dodging. Taylor also had the pleasure of scoring against his old club mates, while Thomas, as on Christmas Day, kicked a quartette of goals.

To-day, Warrington are due at Hull, where they have always a chance. Last January they won 6-5 at the Boulevard after a grand match and I see no reason why they should not repeat the performance if they go in for a fast, open game.

The team for Hull will be Tilley, full back; Jordan Dickenson, Taylor and Brooks threequarters ; Lees and Jenkins, half backs ; Thomas, A. Boardman, Shugars, Naylor P. Boardman and Belton forwards.

THE MESSIAH.

The Warrington Musical Society's rendering of " The Messiah " on Thursday evening was marked by many of those strong points we have grown accustomed to look for in all its work. The grand proportions. the strong light and shade, the all-pervading yet simple dignity of the great Handelian choruses are potentialities of which Mr. Crossley knows exactly how to get his chorus and orchestra to make the most artistic and effective use, and, as a consequence of its general excellence, interest often centred largely in the singing of the combined voices. The precision of the choral singing was admirable, yet something is still left to be desired in the taking up of the parts. The attack may be good, but unless all voices con- cerned participate in it, it is ineffective to the extent that a full tone at the very commencement is thus made Impossible, as was instanced several times on Thursday evening. This is a detail of discipline so easy to be remedied that it is difficult to account for its having escaped attention. The society's fine amateur orchestra was able to deal most effectively with the instrumental parts of " The Messiah " with little or no professional help. Seldom has the Psstoral Symphony been presented with a greater regard for its possibilities in the way of delicate nuances and subtle refinements, while the really splendid way in which Mr. Rutter played the exacting trumpet obbligato on a cornet, was deserving of all praise. Of the quartet of principal vocalists it can scarcely be said that they were more than satisfactory. Some theatricalities in style and expression, and some voice-straining, marred one's ideal or the way in which the parts for the soprano and tenor soloists should be sung, and from this point of view it does not of course count for much that a numerous and enthusiastic Parr Hall audience loudly acclaimed Miss 1, Esta D'Argo's and Mr. Chas. Saunders's sing- ing. ing. Miss Nellie Anderton was clearly overweighted with the profuse orchestration which always more or less handicaps the soloist in "0, Thou that tellest." Her voice though never the most robust, is yet of a most sympathetic quality, and in " He was despised " it was heard not only to advantage, but the deeply devotional character of her singing was conspicuously manifested. Mr. Albert Garcia sang with good taste and expression the music for the principal bass, but he apparently lacks the vigour in voice and style necessary to do full justice to the possibilities of " Why do the nations "and "The trutnpet shall sound." Mr. W. H. Payton. Mus. Bac., at the organ, and Mr. R.H.Pearson at the leader's desk, did yeoman service.

THE" DICE" SEDDON MEMORIAL.

The appeal to the New Zealand public for funds for the " national" memorial to the late Mr. Seddon has (says the "Daily Chronicle ") apart from the railway employes' subscription, evoked but a feeble response. At the end of a year's efforts only £1,165 has been raised, and of this £4OO was collected by the railway servants.

CHRISTMASTIDE.

HOW THE FESTIVAL WAS CELEBRATED AT WARRINGTON.

Of Christmastide and its festivities only pleasant memories now remain. In Warrington as everywhere throughout the land the great home festival has been celebrated with all the old rejoicings and few indeed must have been the homes into which the spirit of Christmas did not penetrate. True the typical " old- fashioned " Christmas of which we read so much in the story books and see more or less faithful pictures of on the Christmas cards and in the magazines has this year been left to the imagination, but the cold snap which was experienced both on Christmas Day and Boxing Day made the conditions much more seasonable than had been expected up to the last moment. To the vigorous walker it was good to be out of doors, the keen air having an exhilarating effect. Christmas Eve saw the streets thronged with a good-humoured crowd which, as usual, had put off making its Christmas purchases until the eleventh hour, and the tradespeople did a brisk business. The waits were abroad too, and the night was filled with music of varying quality from bands whose energetic competition at times produced some strange effects not always welcomed by a kindly. disposed public. For instance, one band was heard discoursing a somewhat dirge-like hymn tune, whilst round the next corner, well within ear-shot, another body of instrumentalists struggled valiantly with "Hail, smiling morn." A. he good old custom of carol singing appears to be on the decline, and the hordes of noisy urchins who too often take the place of the genuine singers create more nuisance than enjoyment. However, when the Christmas spirit in abroad the long-suffering public is very tolerant and some of the " carollers " reaped the desired monetary rewards. Christmas Day was duly celebrated at the Churches by the bolding of services of an appropriately joyous character, and the decora- tion of the buildings with evergreens and flowers were in keeping with the season. The early morning celebrations of the Holy Communion were attended by large numbers of worshippers. In the homes there were the happy re-unions and jovial gatherings around the festive board. That benevolent old gentleman, Santa Claus, had been busy dispensing his bounty, and many a childish heart was gladdened. Those who perforce had to spend the day in public institutions were not overlooked, willing helpers doing their best to make the time pass pleasantly, and Christmas fare was the rule everywerre. On Boxing Day the bright sunshine tempted many people to engage in outdoor recreation and there was a large attendance at the football match at Wilderspool. The proprietors of the amusement houses had made special arrange- ments, and the matinees at the Royal Court Theatre and the Hippodrome were well patronized. In the evening the theatres and the Parr Hall were crowded, the Musical Society giving a highly successful per- formance of" The Messiah " in the latter building. The fact that so many of our townspeople were able to indulge in entertainment would seem to point to a prosperity which is exceptionally welcome at this season above all others. HOT-POT DISTRIBUTION._ _ •

- Though happily there appears to be little acute distress in Warrington, the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) generously arranged for the usual distribution of hot- pot and mince pies to the deserving poor. Tickets had been given out by the clergy and ministers, and the food was served at the Drill Hall between eleven and twelve o'clock to over 500 applicants. The hot-pots had been prepared by Mr. and Mrs. Powys. Amongst those who attended and rendered assistance in the distribution were :—The Mayor and Mayoress (Alder- man and Mrs. Smethurst), the Misses Smethurst, Mr. and Mrs. J. H. Smethurst, Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Forshaw, Misses. Forshaw, Councillor Evans. Inspector Cox (N.S.P.C.C.), Mr. J. Fargher and Mr. W, T. Flood.

- THE INFIRMARY. Everything possible was done for the enjoyment of the inmates of this institution on Christmas Day. Owing to the alterations of the building, which are in progress, there were no decorations. At noon the usual dinner of roast beef and plum pudding was served and thoroughly enjoyed. In the afternoon tea was provided by the nurses whilst in the evening a musical entertain- ment was given by the Misses Sutton and Isherwood. Members of the St. Paul's Church choir also visited the wards and sang carols. Amongst those present during the day were :—Dr. and Mrs. Richmond, Dr. and Mrs. Hodgkinson, the Rev. and Mrs. Bracecamp, Miss Roscoe and Dr. Schofield (Widnes). ORFORD BARRACKS.

To "Tommy Atkins Christmas is the season of seasons, and on Christmas Day he once more entered into the festivities with enthusiasm, the customary special privileges being afforded him and there is no doubt that the soldiers at Orford enjoyed themselves to the highest possible degree. The usual leave of absence had been granted to a great many of the men to visit their homes and friends but to the" stay at homes "the almost deserted ranks were no discourage- ment to enjoyment. The barracks room had been brightened by decorations which made it quite cheerful and gay. The dining hall too, was prettily decorated, seasonable mottoes and greetings to the officers being displayed. At noon the .Christmas dinner of poultry and plum pudding was served and needless to say the men did full justice to it. The evening was spent in dancing, a special floor baying been laid in the room of every company. The music was provided by the depot band, and the proceedings throughout were most successful.

AT THE BLUE COAT SCHOOL, Special privileges were afforded to the children of this well managed school on Christmas day. The school rooms had been tastefully decorated by the children and great praise is due to the Master and Matron—Mr. and Mrs. Rose—and to the teachers,Messrs. G. B.Small- wood and J. L. Millett, for the way the arrangements for the day had been carried out. The day commenced with Divine service at Trinity Church and afterwards dinner, which consisted of roast pork and plum pudding, followed by fruit, was served. In the afternoon when visitors were allowed, games were indulged in and at 5.30 a special tea was partaken of by the children, which they enjoyed to the full. After games had been again played the children retired for the evening.

AT THE WORKHOUSE. The approach of Christmastide is heralded with joy by the Workhouse inmates to whom the day of merry- making comes as a welcome break in the year's routine. This year every effort was again exerted to make Christmas Day a happy time for those who have to seek refuge in this home of poverty, and the festivities passed off most successfully. Evidences of rejoicing were to be seen on every hand. The large dining hall had been most artistically decorated by the labour master, the porter and the inmates, evergreens being sent by Mrs. Lyon, Mr. and Mrs. J. C. Parr and Mrs. Peter Hylands. There were pretty arches of ever- greens from which were suspended baskets of artificial flowers and on the wall appeared the greeting" Health and happiness to our Guardians." The general effect of the decorations was highly pleasing, and did great credit to those who were responsible for the work. The wards of the Infirmary also prkented a bright and cheerful appearance. Willing hands had been busily occupied in embellishing the rooms with tokens of the festive season, and numerous greetings were displayed. All the patients who were fit to partake, were regaled with Christmas fare, and most of them looked happy and welcomed visitors by cheerily wishing them the compli- ments of the season. Some idea of the magnitude of the task of catering for the Christmas feast can be gleaned from the fact • that the numbers provided for were —376 in the Work- hous, and 192 in the hospital. The food consumed con- sisted of 460 lbs. of beef (supplied by Ur. H. Singleton). and 5 cwt. of plum pudding. As usual, the Workhouse inmates attended Divine service in the morning. At noon came the great event of the day—the Christmas dinner—and the old English fare of beef and plum pudding was attacked with avidity. Beer and mineral waters were provided for those who desired them. Mr. and Mrs. Awbery, the master and matron, directed the arrangements and the staff was kept busily employed. Amongst those present during the morning were the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Smethurst), • end the Nlisses Smethurst,"Mr. and Mrs.J. H. Smethurst, Councillor Evans and Mrs. Simpson (Guardians), Mrs. Forshaw and the Misses Forshaw, Miss Henshall, Miss Roberts, Miss Watkins, Rev. W. Bracecamp (chaplain), and Mrs. Bracecamp, Master Bracecamp, Miss Bracecamyi, Mrs. Thorley (Sheffield), and Dr Flitcroft (resident medical officer.) Dinner over, the MASTER addressed the inmates and on their behalf very cordially welcomed the Mayor and Mayoress. (Applause.) It was a cause of great grati- fication to them all that the Town Council re-elected Alderman Smethurst to that exalted position, for althongh they were inmates of the Workhouse those he ' was addressing took a keen interest in the development of the town and had very carefully watched the manner in which the Mayor had discharged his duties. They trusted that his second year of office would be as happy and successful as the first. (Applause.) They took it as a great compliment that his Worship had set the precedent of the Mayor of the borough visiting the Workhouse on Christmas Day. (Applause.) Mr. Awbery expressed regret that the Mayoress had lately not been in the best of health and the pleasure of the inmates that she was able to be with them on that happy occasion. He concluded by moving a hearty vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mayoress. The Rev. W. BRAC EC AMP seconded, and the vote was accorded with ringing cheers. The MAYOR. briefly responded, and said Christmas Day was a day on which they ought all to rejoice and tut° make others happy. He was sure the Master and Matron had done their utmost to make the inmates happy and comfortable. He was told that they had in

the Workhouse two old veterans who had spent the beet part of their lives fighting for Queen and country,and he had pleasure in inviting them to the old people's treat to be held next week. His Worship concluded by address- ing a few practical words to the inmates who at the close of his speech again heartily cheered him and sang "For he's a jolly good fellow." A vote of thanks to the Guardians for providing the Christmas treat was passed on the proposition of two inmates, Mr. Platt and Mrs. Mary Gilligan. Mrs. SIAIINoN returned thanks and said she had been greatly pleased to see how the inmates had enjoyed their treat. That was a day on which they all ought to be happy and to give happiness to others, and they in the Workhouse had had great advantages over many people outside! The Guardians did their best in the interests of the inmates and as far as she could see the officers also worked hard for their benefit and comfort. If the inmates were young they tried to push them on and make them do their best in life, and if they were old they endeavoured to make their days pleasant and comfortable. (Applause.) The gathering then dispersed. In the evening there was a children's Christmas tree, toys having been sent by Mr. T. J. Lee, Alderman Forshaw and Mr. S. Wake- field. A Bohemian concert was also held, presided over by the Master. The men thoroughly enjoyed the luxury of a whiff of the fragant weed, which was one of the privileges of the day. - PADGATE COTTAGE HOMES.

Christmas was not forgotten at Padgate, for it was celebrated with the usual zest in these homes. The four homes presented a bright and cheerful appearance, and judging from the expressions on the faces of the children they were very happy and contented. Each home was decorated with paper flowers and 'holly, and the •week,so far as lessons were concerned,had been a holiday. Amongst the mottoes were the following :—" Health and prosperity to our Chairman and Guardians," "Long live the King " and " Success .to our Leader and Superintendent " ; whilst a similar tribute was paid to their chairman, Mr. George Mather. Both Protestants and Catholics went to their respective Churches, and on their return they partook of their Christmas dinner of roast beef and plum pudding, whilst Mr. William Houghton (chairman of the Guardians) and Mr. George Mather (chairman of the School Committee) discharged the important duties of carvers.

During the afternoon the children were allowed to romp about in the open air, and at five o'clock were all prepared for the good things which had been provided for tea.

in the evening a most enjoyable concert was given by the children. Miss Lily Maddock gave a capital recitation entitled "Suppose," for which she was deservedly encored. Miss Mary Matthews also gave a rezitation entitled " Queer things" ; " Rock-a-bye baby" was also given in a pleasing manner. Great merriment was caused by twenty children who per- formed in a humorous sketch entitled "Our School." —During the day many old scholars were present, and expressed their appreciation of the good work the school had done.

WARRINGTON RAGGED SCHOOL AND MISSION.

A tea consisting of spice loaf, Seed and currant bread, mince pies &c., was provided for over live hundred poor chtldren on Boxing Day by the kind liberality of friends who responded to the Committee's appeal: Councillor Skelland whose unwearied interest in this very valuable institution is most praiseworthy, and ' a staff of helpers were busy all day getting tbe neces- sary meal together and they had their reward in witnessing the happy and cleanly faces around them and in listening to the carolling of the favourite hymn" Glory for me," and hurrahs for merry Christmas. Miss Lawless provided a very interesting and amusing entertainment after tea. Every boy and girl on leaving was presented with a suitable gift from the Christmas tree and an orange. The Committee thank one and all for their contributions to this, the festive gathering of the season.

AT THE POST OFFICE—RECORD BUSINESS'. There has been a tremendous rush at the Post Office this week, :Ind it says much for the resources of the establishment that the immense volume of letters and packages was delivered so expeditiously. Monday was a very heavy day, but the postings of Tuesday far exceeded that day's record. From as early as 8 a.m. until the last collection,missives of all kinds continued to pour in without cessation or appreciable diminution, and required all the efforts of the ordinary staff, aided. by nnmerous outside helpers, who had been specially trained for the purpose several days before, to cope with it. This was successfully done, and it is satisfac- tory to learn that the vast mass of correspondence posted in time, was all cleared away by the night mails. As in previous years, the Post Office uedertook to receive letters intended for delivery on Christmas Day for .more than a week before the event, but an adequate response was not made by the public, as the number so posted fell far short of 20,000, and when it is realized that upwards of 2,009,090 missives were dealt with during the brief period of the pressure, and that a normal average day's posting is 25,000, it will be seen that the result was very disappointing. WORKINGMEN'S MISSION.

The scholars' annual treat in connection with the above Sunday school was held on Thursday and Friday. The senior scholars to the number of 300 assembled in the schoolroom on Thursday night and sat down to a substantial tea, after which a short concert was held, during which the Secretary gave the annual report, which showed that the number of scholars on the books was 892 with an average attend- ance of 534. The adult portion of the school was in a particularly prosperous condition. The Women's class had a membership of 178 an increase of G 6 on last ysar. The junior scholars had a very good average attendance over 200 children being entitled to a book prize. The financial standing of the school was as follows : Balance from last year £1 18s. id, income during year £llO Us. Gd., expenditure during year £lOl 17s. 3d. leaving balance in hand £lO 14s. 4d. Practically the whole of the balance would, however. be required in the course of a week or so, for the purchase of prizes for regular attendance. The above mentioned accounts were exclusive of cost of gas and fuel, which Mrs. Crosfield had, as usual, defrayed. The sum of £lO had also been raised for the support of a native Medicine Dispenser at the Medicinal Mission at Bidar, in the Central Province of India. The Band of Hope and Christian Endeavour Societies had done good work during the year. The children's lantern services had been held each Sunday night during the winter months and had proved very successful. Every Sunday evening the school had been filled to its utmost capacity by hundreds of children, many of whom were not connected with any Sunday school ; on Christmas Eve a treat was given to about 450 poor children. The officers of the school for the coming year are:—superintendents. Messrs.T. Dale and W. Worrall ;, treasurer, Mr. R. C. Tanner; secretary, Mr. A. Gleave ; assistant secretary, Mr. W. Strange ; organist, Mr. F. Atkinson. During the evening, songs were rendered by Miss R. Peacock, and violin solos by Mr. James Watts. Mr. Thomas Dale ably presided over the meeting, supported by Mr. Nathan Hough. Mr. F. Atkinson officiated as accompanist. After the entertainment games were indulged in, and a very enjoyable evening was spent. The junior portion of the school to the number of about 350 had their treat on Friday evening, the happy faces of the children testifying to the fact of their having thoroughly enjoyed themselves.

LIBERALISM IN 1907.

By C. F. G. Masterman, M.P.

r.A.IILIAMENT asseli. unusufy fate -In 1907. The Members had been exhausted by a year's nearly continuous sitting, fol- lowing on a General Election o,f more than normal excitement. The House of Commons came together fresh from the memories of g fierce week before Christmas, in which the conflict with the House of Lords had changed in character from day to day and hour to hour; and after a series of rup- tures, reconciliations, and ruptures again, agreement had been found impossible. It met on February 12th in an angry, uncer- tain frame of mind; bitter at the destruc- tion of its long legislative efforts, yet doubtful of the most efficient means of re- taliation. The King's Speech contained an unusual paragraph of momentous import. "Serious questions affecting the working of our Parliamentary system have arisen," it stated, "from unfortunate differences be- tween the two Houses. My Ministers have &is important .subject under consideration with a view to the solution of the diffi- culty." In the remainder of the Speech a legislative procamme was outlined which made the more experienced Members aghast at its ambition. • First-class bills were promised on the Licensing Question, for the reorganisation of the Army, for Small Holdings and ' for Land Valuation in Scotland; for "further associating the people of Ireland with the management of their domestic affairs," and for the Reform of University Education in Ireland. Other suggestions for legislation included bills for establishing a Court of Criminal Appeal, for Regulating the Hours of Lab- our in Mines, for Amending the Patent Laws, and for a Valuation Bill for Eng- land and Wales, for enabling women to serve on Local Boards, for providing Small Holdings for the rural districts, and for the better housing of the people. Here, it was said, was a programme not for a year but for a decade.

18th. It proved to be a Budget ot promise rather than performance. The Chancellor of the Exchequer definitely announced that It could only be regaraent sug" a preparation for its successor, and should only be judged in connection with its successor. In the happy position of a surplus of over £5,000,000 (due mainly to unexpected mor- tality of millionaires), Mr. Asquith was able to offer a reduction of 3d. in the E (a quarter of- the Income-Tax), upon all earned incomes below £2,000 a-year. This loss was in part to be met by an increase in the scale of Death Duties. A. balance of over £1,500,000 was this year to go to the Debt reduction; but next year, when it was hoped it would have swollen to £2,250,001 it was to furnish a nucleus for a national scheme of old-age pensions. He also pro- posed a reorganisation of the system. of grants in aid for local taxation, abolishing the hall-marking of definite sources of re- venue for this particular expenditure, with a view to obtaining in the future increased revenue for national purposei from Licence Duties and motor-cars.

The Army Bill, meantime, continued its unexciting progress. A " deal " be- tween the two parties ensured its retention in the Committee of the Whole House—a tyromise pleasing to the military Members, who found thus their speeches fully re- ported in the newspapers; but inconveni- ent to the majority of the others, who ex- hibited their unconcern by absenting them- selves from the discussions, and only appear- ing in the divisions. In debates whose audiences rarely rose to eighty the details were thrashed out by specialists; and a great concessiorl on the most controversial point—the retaining of the Militia as a reserve t 44 the Expeditionary Force rather than its incorporation in the Territorial Army resulted in its peaceful passage into law. Interest in the House of Commons was, in fact, at this time deflected to the visit of the Colonial Premiers. They attended in- numerable banquets, promoted by the vari- ous political parties. They made innumer- able speeches, all of a patriotic and Some of a Protectionist nature. Their presence and the long debate at the Conference, in which certain of them pleaded strongly for a policy of Colonial Preference, excited some enthusiasm amongst the Tariff Reformers, and gave rise to a Vote of Censure on the Government for refusing the Colonial "offer." That Vote of Censure was less effective than it might have been from the fact that it failed to unite even the dwin- dled ranks of the official Opposition, and that it was denounced with some vehemence by_ Tory Free Traders on the back benches. _ _ _ _ _

===== 0002601_19071228_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
Interest at first concentrated, however, on the paragraph dealing with the relations between the Houses; and the Prime Mini- ster .made a fighting speech, in which he announced that the Commons would no longer take "lying down" this destruction of their legislative programme. Mr. Bal- four and the Opposition ridiculed a policy which placed a campaign against the Lords at the beginning and Social Reform mea- sures at the end of the programme; and -openly charged them with insincerity in their latter promises. There was also much rather desultory discussion as to a Conven- tion concluded jointly with France in re- spect to the New Hebrides, which was sup- posed to be a violation of the high profes- sions of morality advanced by the Liberals in connection with the Chinese Labour im- portation in South Africa. The criticism was rather cleverly sprung upon the House in unexpected fashion ; and first Mr. Win- ston Churchill, then Sir Edward Grey, were •„compelled to make elaborate replies to ela- borate acusations. The New Hebrides "slavery" furnished picturesque posters for by-elections, but otherwise seemed to. have excited but little interest. After the Debate on the Address Parlia- ment settled down somewhat unexpectedly into a kind of stupor. Time was occupied by long discussions on supplementary esti- mates, and by unfolding of the new Army Schemes by Mr. Haldane, in speeches of ex- • traordinary fluency, preparatory to the in- troduction of his bill. The " Regular " forces were to be concentrated in an Expedition- ary Force" of some 160,000 men, fully .equipped for striking at any portion of the , globe. The Home Defence Force was to comprise a "Territorial Army," made out of the Militia, Yeomanry, and Volunteers, under the control of County Associations, : and with the active support of the Lords- , Lieutenant and the landowning classes.' The plan was attacked by Mr. Arnold- Forster with a personal fury, and criti- •cised, on the whole with a qualified appro- val, by the Service Members. The mass of , -the House absented themselves from the discussions, regarding any Army reorgani- , sation plan with some scepticism and some indifference. The first of the Government measures which appeared was a little bill -(not promised in the Address) introduced by Mr. McKennd under the ten - minutes' rule. It was designed to relieve the con- , science of the passive resister by compell- ing private persons to pay for any denomi- national religious instruction in the non- I provided schools. It seemed to give little , satisfaction to anyone, and painlessly dis- appeared as quietly as it came. At • length, a month after the King's Speech, the first .arrivals of the Government programme came in sight, with the introduction of the Patents Bill by Mr. Lloyd-George and :the Scotch Land Holders Bill by Mr. Sinclair. The former, designed to prevent foreigners artificially crippling British industry by taking out patents in England and refusing to work them, was non-controversial in 'character, and only excited interest in the Opposition through ingenious attempts to demonstrate that it was a violation of the so-called principles of Free Trade. The lat- 'ter, far the most drastic and. far-reaching of the Government's schemes of reform, censuring security of tenure to the Scottish -small holder and the multiplication of holdings where holditigs were desired, was violently attacked by Mr. Balfour, and proved to be the measure of the session most obnoxious to the Tory representatives. Meantime, in the interregnum due to lack of bills, the Prime Minister intro- duced his Reformed Procedure Scheme. The suggestions, like all suggestions for the internal revision of House of Commons methods, excited no kind of interest in the country; they were freely criticised on the Liberal as well as the Conservative side of the House, but they set up a system of machinery, which enabled the Government to harvest no small part of its outlined programme. They es- tablished four Grand Committees in per- manent session, thus (with the House itself -sitting as a Committee) enabling the Com- mittee stages of five bills to be proceeded with simultaneously instead of in succes- sion: It was explained that their purpose would be to deal with non-controversial. legislation. But among them was Included a Scottish Committee, to which were to be sent all que_stions directly affecting Scot- land; and, as this included the Scotch Land Holders Bill, thlit particular Com- mittee Was immediately weighted with legislation of the most acutely contro- , versial character.

The second period—from Easter to Whit- suntide—produced some greater activity, but nothing like the activity which (in the minds of the It_ )re impatient reformers) the necessities of the time demanded. An agitation had arisen in the country in favour of the Government's Land. policy, •culminating in a luncheon attended by the Prime Minister and a great • meeting ad- dressed by Mr. Churchill in Drury-lane. It became evident that in the plan of legislation positions were to be reversed, and the Land Bill, from its place at the bottom of the list, was to appear on the top. The discussion on the new Procedure Rules threatened to drag out intermin- ably, and they were finally driven through, amid the protests of the Opposition, by the gnillotine. The Budget was introduced in .a great speech by Mr. Asquith on April.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Interest in Parliament returned with the Irish Council Bill, introduced by Mr. l3ir- rell before a crowded 'House. It proved to be a much milder affair than' was antici- pated, setting up a partially-elective as- sembly in Dublin to control some of the " Boards " now independent and discon- nected, and to dispose of a considerable sum of money in Irish local finance. It was received by the Irish leaders with qualified approval. In the Whitsuntide re- cess, however, a National Convention, as- sembled in Dublin, rejected it with con- temptuous unanimity;' and the Govern.- ment immediately announced that it would not be proceeded with. No one could deny that the Government assembled for the third section of the Par- liamentary Session with considerably di- minished prestige. Their legislation had either failed to appear or been de- layed, or withdrawn. Their strong words against the Lords had emptied (as it seemed) into thin air. None realised this discontent better than themselves; and from now till the end of the session the House was committed to such an effort of almost savage energy as few sessions of Par- liament have revealed. The majority ac; cepted with rejoicing , the opportunity offered to them. They sat almost continu- ously, day and night; they devoted all their mornings to the new Committees, upon which was piled the detailed conside- ration of measure after measure of reform. When any delay occurred in the passage of their measures through the House, on their return the compartmental closure was freely adopted. The general result of this procedure was that, without resort to an autumn session, the bulk of the Gov- ernment programme was saved; in addi- tion to sundry seemingly derelict bills, which, like the Deceased Wife's Sister Bill, had lain on the roadside neglected for many decades, and now found themselves unexpectedly caught up in the storm and swept into legislative being. The first bill to feel the effects of this lew spurt was the English Small Holdings Bill, which was placed in the hands of Mr. Harcourt. By a mixture of firmness and adroitness Mr. Harcourt succeeded , in steering this bill through the various con- tradictory channels of Opposition, and after prolonged debates in Committee up- stairs, and some futile attempts (which were promptly rejected) to alter it in the Lords, it became law. It provided, for a very grept extension of allotments and small holdings through the joint action of the County and Parish Councils on the one hand, and the Central Boards of Commis- sioners acting under the Board of Agricul- ture' on 'the other. The Scotch Bill, dealing with the same subject on different and far more democratic lines, met with a less happy fate. It was fiercely fought in Com- mittee, word by word and line by line, by skilled obstructionists, mostly English Tory Members; and on reaching the House of Lords it was indefinitely hung up; with a view to such alteration that the Government decided in preference to with- draw it altogether. The Scottish Land Valuation Bill met with a ' similar if less elaborate destruction, being incontinently thrown out in the Lords on the second reading.

Here was the answer of the House Cif Lords (not lacking in courage) to the reso- lutions of the Commons, whia two months before, on June 20th, had at last outlined the Government plan. Before a House crowded from floor to ceiling, and amid breathless interest, the Prime Minister re- vealed the scheme which had been devised, ln eomplicated in character, but de- signing to make the "will of the people" as expressed in the election of a Parliament of particular complexion dominant "within the lifetime of that Parliament." It gave rise to a desultory debate, in which nume- rous schemes of reform were advocated; and after a Labour amendment, which ad- vocated the total abolition of the Lords, had been rejected by 315 votes to 100, the main resolution was carried by a majority of 285- (432 to 147). The Lords took no notice of the resolution, and the further de- velopment of its realisation remained in abeyance for the remainder of the year. All July and August was occupied in 'driving through at eager pace the vari- ous Government bills. Same of the most important, including a Licensing Bill, the Irish University Bill, and the English J....and Valuation Bill were abandoned with- out ever being introduced. But when Par- liament was prorogued on August 28th the salvage from the original programme proved unexpectedly heavy. It included thirty-one Government measures and six measures adopted by the /Government from private Members. The most important bills which 'thus become law (in addition to those already mentioned) were, the bills which set up a Court of Criminal Appeal, which allowed women to sit sn County' Councils and municipalities, which pro- vided for the compulsory registration, of births and the medical inspection of school- children, which brought laundries (com- mercial and institutional) under the con-

===== 0002601_19071228_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
amm

trol of the ieactory Acts, and which at- tempted to settle the long and harassing problem of the Irish evicted. tenants. In the autumn recess interest in the main shifted to Scotland. The two prin- cipal Scottish measures of the session deal- ing with Scotland had been rejected by the Lords. That gave a point to the denunciation of .the Upper House not so directly evident in the sonth. The Labour Members took the field, compensating for • the mildness of their criticisms in the House by the vio- lence of their invective on the platform And amongst the Opposition the Fiscal warfare, always temporarily allayed while Parliament is occupying attention, broke out, as always, anew when Parliament rose. Mr. Balfour, in an invasion of Bir- mingham, laid down a policy which, as on many previous occasions, first provoked a unanimous outburst of enthusiasm, and later provided food for various controver- sial interpretations by the various divided sections of the Unionist Party. In the country all through the year the Government shewed the strength of their unparalleled triumph at the General Elec- tion to be maintained in a remarkable degree. Their poll diminished when they were attacking Tory seats; but in defence they held their own with substantial and in some cases with increased majorities. When a shifting of opinion was manifest, it appeared to be moving rather to the left than the right; at Jarrow and Colne Val- ley Liberal manufacturers were replaced by extreme Socialists; and the general in- crease of the power and number of the Labour Party is certainly one of the out- standing political features of the year. The year's work is thus revealed as leav- ing the Government in perhaps a stronger position than ever. Its particular mem- bers have often shewn quite unexpected ability, and form a very remarkable body of men, with varied individual person- ality. The popularity of the Prime Mini- ster has, if anything, increased. The pos- sibility of any alternative Government has, for the time at least, altogether vanished from the political horizon. The work of Sir Edward Grey at the Foreign Office has called forth a chorus of praise, led by his political opponents; the only criticism corning from a Radical group who deplore a lack of more definite ad- vance in the Congo and Macedonia, or who dislike an entente with the Russian bureaucracy. Mr. Lloyd-George has dis- tinguished hin,iself this autumn by almost sensational victories, achieved by tact and courage' in the ensuring 'of industrial peace. Two years ago it seemed inconceiv- able that a Liberal Government should ever rule England again. To-day it seems equally. inconceivable that England should ever be ruled by anything but a Libera/ Government.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Sunday."

- According to announcement, " Sunday" came on Monday this week, and was warmly welcomed by the patrons of the Royal Court Theatre. It will be remembered that "Sunday " was here at the same time twelve months ago and got both burnt out and snowed up. That fire was a blessing in disguise for it provided Warrington with a vastly improved theatre, and " Sunday" with new scenery. The play is one of the most meritorious and pleasing now on tour, and we never saw it better produced and acted than it is this week. Miss Gertrude Boswell in the title role, is b. most worthy successor to Miss Jean Stirling Mackinley who appeared in the Company on the occasion of its first visit to Warrington, and who has since been act- ing leading parts with Sir John Hare. We anticipated that Miss Mackinley's place would be difficult to fill, and it was an agreeable surprise to us to find the part so excellently played by Miss Boswell. A better " Sunday " could not be desired. She has all the boyish abandon and feminine graces necessary to adequately depict so complex but artless a person as Sunday—and whether in the backwoods with the boys,' or in high society at Brinthorpe Abbey, she is the heroine of the piece par excellence. And "the boys !" What a pleasure it is to see these four rough and ready, honest and hearty American miners in their ocollective position of "father, mother, brother and sister" to Sunday, the orphaned child of an old mate. Towzer, Davy, Lively and Jackey are four unique characters, but one loves them like Sunday does by the end of the play. Mr. James Skea. Mr. Aylmer J. Williams, Mr. J. Hussey, and Mr. Alex Alves reprettent these four rough diamonds in capital style —in fact the " Lively " of Mr. Hussey is masterly. Mr. Ernest A. Douglas is the same Tom Oxley as ever —perfectly at ease and looking the part of the family friend and "good fairy" of the masculine persuasion. In the dual roles of Colonel Brinthorpe and Arthur Brinthorpe, Mr. Leonard Pagden is very successful, keeping the two parts quite distinct with considerable skill both of make-up and manner. Mrs. Naresby (Sunday's English, aunt) is charmingly played by Miss Rosalind Ross, who also makes a capital nun in the first act. " Sunday " has now passed its sixteen- hundredth performance—a fine tribute to the clever writing of its author, Thomas Raceward, and to the capable companies sent out by Mr: Louis Calvert. There will be a matinee and an evening performance to-day.

Sir,—ln your ,last week's column " Spectator " refers to the Warrington Trades Council as being a "dead horse." 1 differ from him, as I believe that a "live ass" would more correctly describe this obstinate inactive bodyi of men, for although they have neither the ability nor inclination to accomplish any useful work, they unfortunately do not possess the ordinary " horse ' sense to resign their official posi- tions and thus allow some intelligent enthusiasts to perform those services which during recent years they have so shamefully mismanaged. 'I wonder if " Spectator " has perused the " Examiner's" report of the Trades Council meet ing held at Friar's Gate on Tuesday, Decem ber 17th. Allow me, Sir, to quote thus :—" When the minutes "of the last meeting came up for consideration Mr. Moorfield said that at the last meeting the Council passed a resolution that the Prudential Staff Association be admitted to the Council. He moved that the resolution be rescinded.' The drastic proposal drew to his feet the" stoical" Mr T. Plumb who then pronounced his Solomonic edict that " they were wasting time if they discussed that resolution, and so induced the timid Mr. Moor field to withdraw his motion and the minutes were then confirmed.

Reading on I notice (after the successful completion of this far-reaching constructive RE-fora) that in response to a letter from the National Federation of School TeachArs (asking the Council's assistance in remedying their grievances in regard to the age of exemption of school children) a resolution was unanimously passed inviting them (the teachers) to become affiliated with the Warring- ton Trades and Labour Council. Messrs. Stott, Plinston and Haley were appointed a deputa- tion to explain the ADVANTAGES and OBJECTS of the Council. Here, Sir, is still another local appli- cation of the well-worn invitation " Will you walk into my parlour? said the spider (Trades Council) tc the fly (Teachers' Association)." No more important business was transacted and the delegates then dis- persed. Great Scott! John, isn't it wonderful the amount of work some people can leave undone with a " clear " conscience?

Allow me, Sir, in conclusion, to inform the members of the Teachers' Association and other unaffiliated organisations that the Trades Council delegates have not, during recent years, given a clear definite lead to their constituents on any burning public question, and alas, they are so densely stupid that they do not even realise the pressing necessity for a Central Trades Hall in Warrington, where all the various local trade union branches could conduct their business and then they wonld be under no obligations to the brewing and publican interests. I am determined, Sir, to leave no stone unturned to bring to a speedy termina- ,4ion these stately exhibitions of masterly inactivity." "ONLOOKER."

THE VOICE OF LABOUR: Edited by John Workman. 31.5.5. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

THE DECLINED CHALLENGE

For five months John Workman has been advocating the theory that each shall give according to his ability and each receive according to his needs. I have been contendKlg that this theory is unworkable. We have argued the question at some length and a fortnight ago I challenged John Wotkman to put his creed into practice, by associating twenty comrades,pooling their earnings and themselves distributing to each according to his needs and with no reference to the individual contribution. I pointed out the conditions surrounding the limited number of twenty (of which he was t) be one) would be more favourable to success, because of its voluntary character than a national compuls )ry system. There is nothing in our present system to prevent this experiment having a fair chance of success—if the principle be right. John Workman construes this ihto a trap and asks "Will you walk into my parlour said the spider to the fly?" If it is a trap at all it is one of John Workman's manufacture because he, not I, has contended for its

practicability, wisdom and justice. I simply asked him to show us that he is sincere in his proposal. He hopes that the Warrington Nursing Association and the Warrington Infirmary (to which I was to contribute £5O each year of the success of the experiment), will not be the poorer by his refusal to accept " such a blundering infringement of God's law" as I would impose upon twenty of his poor deluded brethren. I may have used some strong language in expressing my opinion about John Workman's Socialism but I have said nothing stronger than John Workman's own condemnation when he speaks of it as" a blundering infringement of God's law."

Ana' why he should want to impose this blundering system upon the whole nation it is difficult to say. I know not what to make of John Workman's refusal. We have thought of him, and should like to continue to think of him as a sincere and honest man who believed in his own teaching, but his refusal to put his beliefs into practice indicates an insincerity which is disappointing and perplexing. In conclusion I would wish him the Compliments of the Season and may I say that I think better of him than I do of his creed; and I think on the whole he is wise in declining to try to put it into his deed. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, December 23rd, 1907.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADES COUNCIL INACTIVITY,

Sir, —I wish to add niy "small voice" to the strong denunciatory protests of" Onlooker" and " Spectator ;" and also direct the attention of your readers to the shameful, nay almost criminal, inactivity of the local Trades Council delegates. Whilst the former corres- pondent condemns them for " past " neglects. the latter has evidently not the slightest hope of any improvement in the near future of what he cynically describes as this " Convenient House of Lords "of the Warrington Trades Union movement. I suggest to" Onlooker " that the only way to make this body less inefficient is by substituting "new blood" for the fossilised delegates who now con- stitute the Council.

On the other band, if it can be actually proved that the Trades Council is a "dead horse," then I am willing to assist in removing the "corpse," and I will use what little influence I possess in local trades union circles by recommending my fellow-unionists to at once sever their official connection with the "lifeless" Warrington Trades and Labour Council. After writing the above my attention was directed to the "Examiner's " report of the Council meeting held at Friars Gate last Tuesday week, and from the information there received lam reluctantly inclined to believe that they (the Council) have almost" run their course." R.l.P.—Yours etc., TRADE UNIONIST

RAILWAY NATIONALISATION.

Sir,— The second point raised by Mr. Parker in the reply to his critic seems to many a great obstacle in the way of nationalising our railway. As sure as one discourses upon this question with a friend this burden of the cost is sure to be the foremost objectior. According to the information by my side in 1844 a , Bill was passed through Parliament giving the Government power to acquire the railways at the price of twenty-five years purchase of the annual divisible profits, estimated at the average of the three next pro- ceeding years. The profits of the British Railways for 1905 were £43,446,356. At 25 years' purchase this gives nearly £1,087,000,000. Now the operation is quite simple. The Government will take from shareholders their present share-papers and return them Government paper to the amount of 11,087,000,000. This Government paper will bear a promise to pay interest at the rate of 3 per cent until such time as the Government can buy up the share- paper at its face value. Where is the Government to get the money? Quite simple! Sir Edwin Chad- wick estimates that the multiplicity of railway management raises working expenses 20 per cent.: and this estimate is not too little as can be seen from what has been done abroad. The proportion of work- ing expenses per Mile on the State lines of Germany,l Austro-hungary, Belgium, Denmark, France, Italy, Norway, Holland, Roumania and Russia, to the expenses of the private companies, in the same countries, is on the average, in the ratio of 9 to 7. Two-ninths of the -private com- panies' expenses are thus saved by the State Railways through unity of management—two-ninths equals about 24 per cent.,so that 20 percent. is within , the mark. The working expenses of our railways for 1905 were about £70,000,000, 20 per cent. of that is £14,009,900 per annum. Now add £14,000,000 saved annually to the £43,446,356 annual profits, tohl £57,500,000. But £32,410,000 has to be paid in , interest, which leaves us with a balance in hand of £25,090,000. Over £25,000,900 to annually buy out the stockholders. Roughly, then, we could, without Itouching a penny of the national purse, in about 35 years, gain possession of the railways. This is not a scheme emanating from a mind always amongst the clouds. The cold logic of the case for railway nationalization, the meanest intellect can perceive The simple manner in which it can be carried out' perhaps even Mr. Parker will now admit.—Yours &c., LABOR OMNIA VINCIT.

Funeral of an old Forester and Ex-Constable. Mr. William Wheatley, who was always called " Dad," by the members of the Warrington Police Force was carried to his last resting place on Sunday last by senior con- stables of the Force. Born in the county of Bucks in the year 1840, a policeman's life had a charm for him, and he joined the London Metropolitian Police when they wore the regulation dress of top hats and cutaway coats. While he was connected with the London force the first underground raiLvay was made. Some of his comrades asked him to put some money in the new railway and his reply was, he did nc, intend to put his savings underground. After 8 years service with the London Metropolitian Police he connected himself with the Buckingham County Police. He joined the Warrington Police in February 1872, lie served 24 years and was superannuated in 1896 ; he was highly respected by his. fellow constables and by many inhabitants of the town. Upon coming to Warrington he joined Court Hope 431, of this District of Foresters and held all offices in connection with with the Court to which he belonged, and up to the last always took a great intereA in the welfare of the Order. Amongst those who attended his funeral as a mark of respect were the District Chief Ranger, Bro. Walter Smith, and , the Sub-District Chief Ranger, Bro. J. Walsh, 2 District Trustees, 6 past District Chief Rangers and several past Chief Rangers. There were representa- tives from all the Foresters' Courts in town. At the graveside Bro. G. Maybury, P.D.C.R., read the Foresters' Funeral Service which was very impressive.'

STORY COMPETITIO

:LDREN,

S Thank you all very much for your kind letters and contributions. What a lot there are, to be sure, and still the postman keeps on bringing me either a letter, a card, or a parcel. Now, -1 am sure you are all anxiously waiting to see who has won my splendid Christmas prizes; So if you just glance at the top of the next column you will find the full list. Such a time I have had going through the piles and piles of competition papers, the entries this Christmas being greater than ever—and I have had to spend many hours in carefully reading every piece, however small. The Pcetry Competition has been very popu- lar. and also the Christmas Story Competition. Lots of jokes and cc,nundiums were sent in, but the standard of merit did not reach as high as the first two mentioned.

In the Poetry Competition you sid/1 see that the first prize has been carried off by Jennie Bushby. Jennie's poem, "The Waits," was very neatly written, and cleverly illustrated. I wish you could all see it. lam hoping to print another contribution from this little girl before so keep your eye on the " Corner." I am sure that Florence Couling, who is only just seven years old, has done well to carry off the second prize. Of course, her roem was written in a big, round hand, so that the printer's man could read it ever so far off, but. I am sure it's much better than I could write when I was seven, which is a long, long time ago. My little nephew, Sohn Lister, has forgotten to put his age, but I guess he isn't much older than Florence. Just fancy your Uncle reading all the jokes that were sent in. I have been kept. smiling all the day long. lam sorry .that the editor cannot reproduce the very funny drawing which Wilfred Padley sent. to illustrate his joke. I shall expect some more sketches from Wilfred, who, by the way, is only eleven. Some day I may be able to put one or two draw- ings in the paper, but at preSent we cannot spare the room. I hope Cissy Fletcher will be pleased with the nice box of chocolates I have sent to her address. Befcre I close my letter this week. I must tell yo a about one thing that pleased me very mach about these competitions, and that. was to notice so many little tots enter- ing for prizes.. In fact, I have Leen so delighted with. the careful 'and neat way in which many of these young members have done their work that I have sent special prizes of nice packets of Fry's Chocolate to the following, none of whom are more than five years old :—Willie Fisher, Lizzie Brothwell, Edith Holland, Xrthur Brothwell, Maggie Leys, Annie Yorke, Elsie Wells.

Ist PRIZE-STORY COMPETITION. A KINDLY ACT.

In one of our large cities a bazaar was being held to raise a certain amount of money to provide a little comfort and cheer to the poor of the slums of that city on Christmas Day. On the day of the bazaar •the weather was very cold, and snow lay on the ground. Ladies and gentlemen came to the bazaar in their carriages and motor-cars. Amongst the visitors were Lord and Lady Worldly, accompanied by their only daughter, a beautiful child of nine years, with her nurse in attendance. This nurse had been brought up in a good home, and was taught to honour God, and love her fellow creatures, whatever their lot in life. may be. Whenever she had an opportunity, she would tell her little charge about her Lord and Master, Who, when on earth, went about doing good unto the sick and poor. Lord and Lady Worldly were known to be people of indifference a, regards religion. Amongst the many beauti- ful things they boull was a large doll, which their child had exp sed a desire to obtain, and when they had finished buying they made their way back to their motor-car; the child walked behind her parents,' holding the doll in her arms. Just as they were about to get into the car, two little ragged children ran up to watch them. One of them was a small girl whose clothes were of the thinnest description, and whose little pinched face beamed up with ambition as she saw the large doll dressed in such beautiful clothes. The owner of the doll saw the child come up to the car, and, iremem- bering what her nurse had taught her, with tears in her eyes she placed the doll into the ragged child's arms. When her parents saw the act of their daughter, their ccnscience smote them, and it is reported that many a poor home was made a lot brighter that Christmas- time, through the little child's kindly act to one of Christ's little ones. WILLIAM R. FARRAR.

NOW I am sure you will all be pleased to read the many contributions this week, and remember that those Who have not gained any prize will receive a nice little pocket mirror, as a small Christmas gift. CLOTHING FOR THE POOR CHILDREN. There is no doubt in my mind that there are plenty of people with kind hearts,'-ready and anxious to help those in poverty. I have been most pleased with parcels of warm clothing to hand during the present week. I do not want to take up much room, but I must give you details of the case lam helping this week. The senders of clothing will be happy in knowing what immense help their kindness has been to this poor family during the present cold and wintry weather. In a very small cottage in a city live husband and wife and nine children. The mother is ill, and one little girl has to st,op at home from school to nurse her. This poor little child has been getting on well at 'school, and would have taken the first prize for attendance and lessons for the whole year, but, unfortunately, she has now lost this owing to being forced to remain at hoime with a sick mother. The father is tramping the streets for work; the factory where he, was employed is slow' and has discharged hands. The eldest of the family—a young matt of twenty, formerly engaged in wire working, is out of work, and has to remain at home, owing to his, clothes being in rags; he is at present unable to leave the tiny room owing to lack of clothes to wear. The rest cf the children are in rags. and have nothing but bread to eat, and not enough of that. This is the case my kind little members are helping. Ido not, wish to go further into details of this family's poverty. I think enough has been said to show that your kind thoughts and actions will give much pleasure and warmth this Christmas. Articles have come to hand from G. M. Clark, Bessie Lewis, Nellie Bray, Gladys,Groves. Gertrude Clark, Ida Bow, Florence Kidell, Doris Knight, Constance Knight., Gladys Knight, Dorothy Starke, Margery Horkley, and Master Knight. A large parcel has just come to hand, which, will be acknowledged next week. A special word of thanks to Mrs. J. Wells, who has been mast kind in giving assistance. UNCLE NED.

CHRISTMAS CARDS AND WISHES. Willie Scott, Margery Hockley, ,Florence Kivett, Ida Bow, H. Copp.

TRIPPING INTO TOWN.

A little lass with golden hair,

A little lass with brown, A little lass with raven locks, Went tripping into town. "I like the golden hair the best!" "And I prefer the brown!" "And I the black!" three sparrows said, Three sparrows of the town. " Tu-whit Tu-whoo!" an old owl said, From the belfry in the town; "Glad-hearted lassies need not mind

If locks be gold, black, brown. Tu-whit ! Tu-whoo! so fast, so fast,

The sands of life run down. And soon, so soon, three white-haired dames Will totter through the town; Gone, then, for aye, the raven locks, The golden hair, the brown; And she will fairest be, whose face Has never worn a frown!"

CONNIE FIII,LEE.

2nd PRIZE—STORY COMPETITION.

LITTLE GERTIE'S GIFT.

Gertrude Flanders was the daughter of a very rich lady and gentleman. Gertrude was nine years old. She was a beautiful child, with long flaxen hair and blue eyes. It was Christmas Eve, and she had received a letter from an uncle, enclosing a pound as a little pocket-money for her to spend at Christmas-time, and now she was sitting on the rug before the fire, thinking how she should spend her sovercikn. Presently she jumped up and ran to find her mother. Mrs. Flanders was sitting in the drawing-room. Lusy sending off Christmas-cards, etc. On hearing Gertie enter the room, she looked up, and said, "Well, Gertie, dear, what do you want?" "Mamma," said-6ertie, " I have been think- ing how I could spend that sovereign Uncle William sent me, and at last I thought I should like to-buy a nic3 doll and a perambulator to wheel her about in. I came to ask you if you would take me out to look in the shop windows and see if there was a doll I should like."

Mrs. Flanders said she was going out in about half-an-hour's time, and that Gertie could go with her then.

Accordingly, when Mrs. Flanders was ready, she and Gertie started out. It was Mr. and Mrs: Flanders' custom. to give away a lot of things every winter to the poor. Mrs. Flanders first took Gertie to see a poor widow lady with three children. The eldest child was about seven years old, the next about five, and the youngest was four. Mrs. Flanders did not stop long. After promising to send some good things to them, she bade them good-bye. When they had gone a little way down the street, Gertie said to her mother, "Mamma. I noticed that Mrs. Slade's children had not any toys to play with; so I have been thinking that if I went without my perambulator. I could give the eldest daughter a doll, and the next one a picture-book. and the baby a rag doll. What do you think, Mamma?" Mrs. Flanders said it was very thoughtful of Gertie to think of the poor children's wants. They had just arrived at a nice, big toy shop, and here they agreed to make their purchases. When Gertie had bought her doll and, the things she had mentioned- in the street to her mother. she found she had still a few Shillings to spare, so she decided' to send them to Mrs. Slade to help to buy, some of the many things they requited. You may guess how delighted the Slades were when a, parcel arrived for them with all those nice things, in it.

You may also guess how delighted Gertie was when she woke up on Christmas morning and saw the doll she, had bought, beautifully dressed, lying in a- perambulator by the side of her bed, for her mother had determined to give her a little surprise as a reward for her act of self-sacrifice. MARGARET MARY PRESS.
