THE STRANGER WITHIN THE GATE. -

BY FRED M. WHITE,

author of "The Cardinal Moth," " Tregar. then's Wife," "The White Glove," "It. Fateful Face," &C.

The wind howled in the chimney so that the sparks from the wood fire raced upwards like millions of stars to meet the fury of the night. Then the gale was lulled for a moment, a strange white silence followed, and once again the rush of snowflakes on the win- dows.

Hetty Dane drew closer to the fire and Shivered slightly. She could .find it in her heart to wish that all the girls were back at school now, though term-time was by no means a primrose path for her. Yet it was so lonely in the great gaunt house that she would even have 'welcomed Miss Adela and Julia Grimshaw back to their own fireside again. But those austere ladies were not likely to return just yet. Indeed, so far as Hetty could see, their homecoming was likely to be postponed for some days, seeing that the snow had been falling steadily for twenty- four linT s now; and there was a prospect be- fore Hetty of passing an exceedingly solitary Christmas. The Misses Grimshaw had is- parted leaving many rigid instruction's behind them. They had intimated to Hefty that, if the weather did not become more propitious, they would probably stay in Castleford and pass . Christmas with a brother who lived

there. Well, they would not return. Hetty had made up her mind on that point. As to the solitary Christmas, she cared little or nothing. There had been no festive season so far as she was concerned for some years past. Still, it was just a little unfortunate that the, ope -ster.vant remaining in the house should have sprained her ankle, and thus necessitated a Stay in bed. Hetty would have to d'o all the work, but that she would not mind. She stood there before the fire, dreaming and wondering how many Christmases the old house had seen. It was a grand old place in its way, and at one time had been in the pos- session of an old county family, whose name was still remembered in the North. Doubt- less, in times gone by, many a Yule' log had blazed itself away upon that massive hearth, many a time had King Christmas held high revel there. Now it was all prim and bare, as befitted a ladies' school, cold and polished as the hearts of the two women who governed that select establishment. _ .

For some time Hetty stood there, until at length she became conscious of the fact that someone was knocking at the front door. It was a somewhat feeble knock, only half heard above the fury of the gale; and, just for a moment, it occurred to Hetty that she was mistaken. Then the knock came again, fol- lowed by a groan. Scared and startled, Hetty hastily crossed the hall, and 'threw the door open. Had not she done this, had she given herself time to hesitate, she would probably have been afraid to do anything of the kind. She forgot all her fears now, as she saw the figure of a man on his hands and knees in the deep snow which had collected on the doorstep. A furious blast of wind set the old house humming, and a fine powder of snow drifted across the polished boards. With sudden strength that quite astonished herself, Hetty dragged the man inside and pushed the door to again. She had to fight against 'the strength of the gale before she succeeded. When she turned er attention towards the newcomer, she not fail to -notice how dreadfully pal and drawn his features were. There wasd'an ugly cut on the forehead from which the blood was flowing "Sit

freely. "You are badly hurt," Hetty said. down here, will you?" She pushed an arm3hair up to the .fire, ard the stranger literally fell into it, without uttering a word. He appeared .to be abso- lutely dazed and overcome; for some mo- ments it was evident that be had not the- slightest idea where he was. Then, gradu- ally, as the grateful heat of the fire restored hini to something like life, again, he turned to Betty and smiled. It wag a pleasant, frank

which gave an attractive expression to a countenance which was not exactly hand- some, yet pleasing. "I am afraid I am giving you a lot of trouble," he said. "The fact is, I was going on to Castleford for to-morrow, and our train got snowed up in a cutting. There was a bit of an accident, and that was how I came to be hurt. I believe nobody was damaged but myself, 'so that when the guard came and told us we could get no further, everybody turned out of the train and went off to find accommodation in the village. I suppose I was a bit too much knocked to keep up with the rest. Any way, I lost touch with them, and when the last breath. of the blizzard came on I might have been miles away from help for all I knew to the contrary. I saw your light here, and, more by good luck than any- thing else, I managed to reach the 'house. I am sorry to trouble you." "It is no trouble at all," iletty said. "If you will sit here by the fire I will get some warm water to bathe your head. I daresay we can manage to make you comfortable for the night, but haven't you a bag or portman- teau or something of that kind?" The stranger replied that he fancied he must have left it on the doorstep. And, _

sure enough, there it was when Heti), opened ti;i door again. Before half-an-hour • had p sed her visitor was quite himself again. He seemed .to have a wonderful faculty for . . . . _ .

obtaining information without [taking -ques- tions, and before Hetty realised that she had told him anything, he knew all about the Misses Grimshaw, to say nothing of the un- fortunate servant and her sprained ankle. Li a calm, assured sort of way, he took the situation in his own hands. He might have been master of the house.

"This is a terrible dilemma," he said, with a smile. "It is my plain .duty to take myself off at once. but I am not. in the least inclined to do anything of the sort. I daresay I shall be able to find some sorts of conveyance to- morrow. If you will be good enough to let me have two. or three rugs I can make myself quite comfortable before the tire." • Iletty rathor demurred to this, but George Barton was quite firm. He would keep up the tire, he said, so that .11etty would not have to light it on the following morning. When she did come down next day, something. in the Way of a surprise greeted her. There was a roaring fire in the hall, another had been made up in the dining-room, and the kettle was boiling gaily in the kitchen. Moreover, the breakfast things were . laid, and on the Old-fashioned hob by the dining-room fire stood a dish of fresh eggs and bacon, flanked by a pile of steaming buttered toast. With s gay smilo Barton pointed to his handiwork. I hope you don't mind," he said. 'of 2ourse, I know it -is rather a liberty, but when I woke up this morning the fire ,was out and I was literally frozen. Then I thought of you and your damaged servant, and well, really,' there was nothing else for it." Hetty laughed aloud, such a laugh as the eld house had not heard for years. It never eccurred to her-that her companion was look- ing at her with frank admiration in his blue eYes- George Barton was enjoying the situa- tion.

"I daresay you wonder how I Managed it,:' he , said. " But, you see, I am used to this kind of thing. I have been a solitary man all my life, and . for the last four or five years it has been a whim of mine to live in a tiny cot- tage, not far out , of London, where I do everythin. myself. Ido all my own house- work and my own cooking, though I dare- .BllY you would not approve of some of my methods. At any rate, this is .done now, aim I sinceely hope you , will not think I have been taking a liberty.'' "Indeed, no," Betty cried, warmly. "I think it is exceedingly kind*, and thoughtful. But don't you think " It is a pity to let the breakfast get cold," Barton- laughed. "Afterwards I csn go and prospeet. round and see .if I can find some avenue to the village." jtarton eame back presently with a soffi eiently grave face and information to the effect that the snow round the house had drifted to a height of teu or Ape hut, aimi

WARRINGTON AfiRIaILTURAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Agricultural Society was held at the Masonic Hall, Bold-street on nonday afternoon Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., presiding overa large attendance which included Mr. Neilson (vice-chairman), Mr. Alfred Broome (treasurer), and Mr. H. Beresford Melville (secretary). A letter of apology for non-attendance was received from Colonel Legh who is away from the neighbour- hood.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESIDENT.

Mr. T. BAXTER moved the re-election of Sir Gilbert Greenell as Pre3ident of the Society. Walton Show without Sir Gilbert would be like Hamlet" without the Prince. He was an ideal president. Mr. DAVIES (Warburton) seconded and said the farmers in Lancashire and Cheshire were much indebted to Sir Gilbert for his encouragement of agricultural societies. Year after year he handsomely supported the Warrington' Show but if they could be favoured with fine weather there was no reason why the show should not be self-supporting. The resolution was heartily carried. Sir GILBERT GREF.NALL said he would be most happy to act as president again. He did not see the least reason why the show should not be self-supporting. They had never really had a fair chance since they had been on the new ground; they had had nothing but a series of wet days. He hoped next year they would be more lucky and have a real chance of trying whether they could make the show pay or not. He did I not mind having to pay the money as there was-no doubt it did a great deal of good in the neighbourhood, but he did not think it ought not to be necessary and if they had a fair trial he believed they would be able to prove it was not necessary to have outside help. VICE-PRESIDENT. llt was decided to ask Colonel Richard Pilkington to become the Vice-President of the Society.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
THE YEAR'S WORK.

The SECRETARY presented *annual report and after a reference to the success of the 18th annual .; show despite heavy rain, said :-From a study of the , annual balance sheet, it will b noticed that the figures I throughout compare very- avourably with previous ' years. The amounts shown for Show Yard and printing are a little higher, but the accounts are not debited with the whole cost of the erection of the Show Yard, a large portion of this being carried out by the Presidents Estate Staff, and not charged for. It will therefore be seen that except for the great help given by Sir Gilbert Greenall the society would not be self-support- ing, which is a matter of the greatest regret. A special donation of £l9O to meet the deficit was again given by Sir Gilbert. The Flower Show with which Lady I Greenall's name is always associated, is a very pleasing ' and attractive addition, and certainly attracts a number of visitors who might otherwise stay away. The whole of the prizes are given by Lady Greenall. The Vice- President for the year was Colonel Leigh, and he gave a Silver Cup as a special champion prize in the Cattle Classes. These classes show a steady improvement both as regards number and quality, and the increase has justified the Council in taking the Society's Dis- trict further into Cheshire and more into the larger breeding area. No comment need be made, and it would certainly be out of place to criticise the excel- lent Judging of the Shires. The high standard of quality keeps up, but unfortunately numbers do not increase, and it is always a matter of disappointment i that all the breeders in the District do not take advan- tages of the facilities given of bringing their stock , before the buyers. Undoubtedly the Warrington Show lhas made its mark, and it is a well kuowe fact that I much stock changes hands after the local event. Much satisfaction was again given by the prompt and efficient manner in which the stewards carried

out their work. In no single case was a steward found to be absent from the post allotted to him, and they have most willingly fallen in to the system, and materially helped in the successful carrying out of the arduous duties connected with the day's show. ,In a few cases they were on duty for close upon twelve hours. The Council again invite co-operation from all interested in agriculture, and for the special support of the Society. The membership doe.; not increase as it might, and as work of the kind cannot be satisftc- torily carried on without financial support, a larifer number of members would be of assistance not only for the amount of their inbscriptions, but for the general support in other directions. The great question of the close of entries has been considered from time to time by the .Council, but unfortunately there is not much improvement, and great inconvenience is given by late entries, which cause much delay in getting out the catalogue. Intending exhibitors are agaitl asked to endeavour to • send in their entries as early as possible, but in any case certainly not later than the day appointed by the Society as the last day of entry. Sir Gilbert Green3ll has again Invited the Society to. Walton for the 1903 show, and the Council have arranged to hold the show on Thursday 13th August, 1903. The report was unanimously adopted. THE FINANCIAI, POSITION.

and the President had given a speci;il donation of £l9O, so that they had now £32 Is. 2d. in hand. The statement was accepted. ' THE COUNCIL.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible, not later than Thursday.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
Socialism as Applied to Charitable Trusts.

"Whether or no confiscation applied to trusts is Socialism is a question that need not perhaps be con- sidered. If it is, however, then there is, in my opinion, little to choose between the Socialism of the Govern- ment and the Socialism of Mr. Kier Hardie and his friends." The above words were used in a letter sent to the 41 Warrington Guardian" last week by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, and they are not, to say the least, very credit- able to him. There are some very noble men connected with the Established Church, for whom the Socialists have the greatest respect. In fact some of its ministers are avowed Socialists, but this must not deter the Socialists of Warrington from retaliating, when they are attacked in the above odious manner, with all the material at their command, and they should, in my opinion, ignore the positions held by their opponents in the Church. Robert Blatchferd says Socialism is a policeman, not a thief." Mr. Carleton is very much concerned about trusts and is never tired of writing to the Press endeavouring to prove that property left for a specific purpose, prior to the year 1703, must now, and for all eternity, be used for that particular object. I have just been reading the" case for the Disestablish- ment "and according to this book the Church Estab- lishment is .supported by an annual revenue of £5,753,557, only £284,386 or which is derived from private benefactions since 1703. In other words the Church takes indirectly from the workers of this country every year £5,753,557, in the shape of rent, interest and profit. The book above mentioned also tells us that thirty four Bishops receive annually .£164,000,and that when these superfluous personages die they leave on an average £50,000 each. The average stipend of the Bishop is £4,800 ; a well paid artisan willnot receive more than £BO per annum. If this artisan pat the whole of his wages in the bank, it would just take him 625 years to accumulate £50,000, the amount which the Bishep leaves when he dies. In the face of the above facts how can Mr. Carleton talk of morals or religion. Nobody in his right mind will contend that the Bishops are worth anything like the amount of money they receive, and if they do not earn their stipends, is it either moral or religious for thera to accept the money? The fact that they die rich men proves that they don't " sell all that they have and give to the poor." Really when we notice how keen and interested these gentlemen are in regard to material things and how uninterested in regard to spiritual things, we cannot help but think that their conduct is responsible to a great extent for the irreligion of the present day. If the Established Church had been as religious, or moral, as some of its representatives would have us believe, it would have used its influence to remedy some of the great social evils in our midst. la this Christian (?) country of ours, with its boast of all its wealth, we have children dying in thousands through insufficient nourishment, and insanitary dwellings; we have men and women committing suicide through our rotten competitive system ; we have twelve millions of our people always on the verge of starvation; we have thousands of women selling themselves body and soul to enable them to get bread to live, and a host of other indescribable evils. Now I ask Mr. 'Carleton what has the Bench of Bishops done, and what is be doing, to alter this condition of affairs, which is both immoral and irreligious? The Bishops have not up to the present given one vote out of ten on behalf of the workers of this country; on the other hand Mr. Kier Hardie has devoted the whole of his life endeavouring to make the lot of the worker brighter and happier. Had the Bishops been as religious or moral as he, and had they worked as hard for social reforra, we should not now be flghtin., in London for the feeding of necessitous school children.—Yours,

A SOCIALIST.

Is Interest Robbery ? Sir,—l am glad to see the letter of Mr. George Reeves in your last week's issue and recognise its courtesy. cannot follow Mr. Reeves in his statement that the present form of ownership of capital divides society into two great classes. In the same issue in which the letter of Mr. Reeves appears is an account of the annual meeting of the Warrington Savings Bank. In this institution there are 15,465 accounts open of depositors the aggregate amount of whose savings is £407,587 Its. 7d. The dividing of society 'into two great classes seems to me to be evolved out of Mr. Reeves' imagina- tion ; at least I canuot trace the division. By the way, would Mr. Reeves say that these depositors are robbers in so far as they are getting interest on the savings? No doubt the bulk of these investors are wage-earners, the proletariat as Mr. Reeves calls them, but how does he class them, and does he draw the dividing line above or below this class? The fact is we are all workers (or ought to be) and we are all capitalists. Even the Socialist who has nothing stored in Savings Banks, Co-operative Friendly Societies,or Trades Unions,is a capitalist every week-end He is an employer of labour when, say, with his 235. he goes to market to see how much labour he can corn- mend. By experience he learns where to go to get the most value in labour products, his desire is to get as much of the produce of labour as he can and give as little of his labour product in exchange as possible. We will now consider Mr. Reeves' more definite answer to my request to be shown why interest is robbery, say in the illustration given of the hired horse and cart. The answer is as follows :—" The real value of an article is its cost of production. The wear and tear are caused by the user of the horse and cart, and the cost of the loss on the part of the lender would be the legitimate price to charge the user of the horse and cart. Should any other interest be charged it would be put upon the cost of production and the consumer would be charged a price over the real value, and I suppose anOne who does not get value for money would say he had been robbed." Ido not think the first sentence is strictly true. For example, suppose a garment made by hand takes 36 hours to make and the unit for labour per hour is 4d., the labour cost would be 12s. ; suppose the seamstress .can with the help of a sewing machine make the same .article in 24 hours the labour cost would be Bs. If the seamstress had hired the machine and paid in interest Is., the labour cost wuuld be Os. That is, by the help of capital (the machine in this case), 3s. is saved. We have therefore two garments, one of which cost in labour unaided by capital 125., the other aided by capital' 9s. Assuming that both garments are equally durable surely the real values are equal. There is no doubt, as Mr. Reeves states, that interest is produced by labour, but in this case as in others like it, the interest of extra value is produced by aid of the labour put into the machine by the maker, and not by the user of the machine. In his answer Mr. Reeves puts this interest on to the cost and makes the con- sumer, in this case the wearer, pay it in the value of the article, and I presume the maker of the garment keeps the extra Is. in addition to the Os. But what I wish Mr. Reeves to make clear is why the maker of the machine is not to be rewarded for his toil as well as the seamstress. Mr. Reeves will probably say, as be does in the horse and cart illustration, that If the maker of the machine gets his machine back plus wear and tear that this is the only legitimate charge that can be made' because he gets back intact his labour product. But this argument overlooks the fact that the machinist might have used his labour and time in making a machine or some other thing for his own use which would have increased his labour product during the time that the machine has been lent to another worker. The Socialists' attack on interest seems therefore to be an attack on labour and an attempt to tafte fram it its legitimate reward. If I am right are not the tables turned and is not the Socialist plan that of the robber? With a good deal of what Mr. Reeves says about present conditions lam in accord, probably there is less difference between us than he imagines, but before we proceed to discuss other aspects of the question it would be well to clear up one point at a time and to consider it separately and independently, and, having put my difficulties,' would invite him to explain a little more clearly why he considerd interest robbery. For the time being let us keep the question of the ethics and law of interest apart from that of land, rent, and profit. These can, if desired, be discussed in their turn and their relations with interest corelated.

I recognise that if this discussion is to throw any light on the truth and light which all men are feeling after in respect to social questions it will be necessary to closely ex‘.inine the ethical and economic foundation on which the statement that " Interest is robbery" rests. In this endeavour I welcome Mr. Reeves' co-operation, but I recognise that any lengthy corre- spondence on economics which does not bear immediately on the particular phases of thought which are uppermost in the minds of those interested in Socialism will be considered dry and read with impatience; to adopts colloquial expression it does not touch the spot. But we will come to this later and come better equipped after we have cleared the ground and examined the base. Socialists would have us discuss the top storeys while in our opinion it is more necessary to examine the foundations. Be assured that if these are faulty the building however pleasing it may look, will be unsafe. It is my desire to shorten this part of the correspond- ence and possibly Mr. Reeves .can help me in this by recommending sone Socialist works which deal with, say, the question of interest being robbery in a logical end thorough manner. I have been a reader of Socialistic literature for the last thirty years, getting all the works that came within my reach, but I must confess to have found very little that grips the subject and lays a solid foundation upon which the structure of which we hear so much can safely rest. C. E. PARKER. Panketh, December 23rd, 1907.

He thought it most unfortunate that such a national game as Rugby should be under the charge of two distinct bodies. VISIT TO KNUTSFORD.

G. T. P. Warrington New Zealand 2 1 7

On Sunday the New Zealanders visited Knutsford, where they were entertained by the Warrington Club to tea at the Royal George Hotel. AT MESSRS. CROSFIELD'S WORKS.

On Monday morning a visit to the Bank Quay Works took place, and the visitors expressed themselves pleased with all they saw. The girls in one of the departments sang several choruses, whilst the Colonials gave the Maori war song. The visitors returned to Manchester at 12.30. THE ALL, BLACK'S' RECORD.

RECEPTION AT THE TOWN HALL.

The New Zealanders arrived in Warrington at Bank Quay Station at 11.20 on Saturday morning, the arrange- ment to come by the Cheshire Lines being cancelled at the last minute. There was a good crowd to receive them, and on the platform Mr. Baskerville and his ,party were met by Messrs. Warren (president), Ashton (secretary), Pemberton (treasurer), and other officials and members of the Warrington Football Club. A four- horse 'bus conveyed the visitors to the Town Hall. where they were received in the vestibule by the Mayor and Mayoress,Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, the Town Clerk, Alderman Bennett,Alderman Tinnion and others. . .

A cordial welcome was given the New Zealanders, The Nlayor and Mr. Warren both making a few remarks, which were replied to by Ndr. H. Wright (captain), and Mr. J. Gleason (treasurer) of the New Zealand team. This short ceremony over, the tourists drove to the Lion Hotel for lunch, preparatory to the match. HIGH TEA AT THE LION HOTEL.

PRESENTATION OF CUP-HOLDERS' PORTRAITS. After the match the teams, officials, and friends sat down to a high tea at the Lion Hotel. Mr. Warren pre- sided, and among the guests present were the Mayor, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Mr. Robert Pierpoint, Mr. E. Draper, Alderman Tinnion' Alderman Evans and Councillor Fulton-Smith. The handsome Northern Union Cup, of which Warrington are the holders, occupied a prominent place on the President's table,and was much admired by the" All Blacks." Mr. H. Ashton read letters apologizing for inability to attend from Mr. A . H. Crostield, M.P., the reason for which, ha said, they all knew,and could sympathise with him (Laughter) ; Mr. James Dodds, who had been forbidden by the doctor ; Mr. J. Lyon Whittle and Alderman Pierpoint. • The toasts of " The King" and "The Queen and Royal Family "were proposed from the chair, and drunk with musical honours. THE NEW ZEALAND TEAM.

MR. WARREN AND THE PROFESKONAL GAME.

The PRESIDENT, in proposing "the New Zealand team," recalled the visit of the first team of "All Blacks,' and said it was a disputed point to-day whether the present team was as good as the one that visited two years ago. At that time there was a lot of correspondence in the papers and private talk outside as to what the Northern Union teams would have done with the" All Blacks." They of the Northern Union thought the Rugby Union clubs had very much degenerated and were not uphedding the true traditions of Rugby football, and they were itching to have a smack at the New Zealanders. The Colonials, however, were forbidden to meet professionals. While those terribly fierce encounters were going on between the present New Zealand team and the N.U. clubs, it was not so much that they aimed at beating the visitors as that they were fighting and trying to draw a line through the Colonials and the old Rugby Union clubs, and the feeling had been caused principally by the way the Rugby Union had so contemptuously treated them as professionals. He hoped to see the day when that feeling would be waived aside, and the N.U. clubs would be able to meet the togby Union clubs in friendly rivalry. After all there would have been littie good football left in the North of England had it not been for the Northern Union. (Hear, hear.) The game was a dying one when they took it in hand,and they had brought it into very vigorous life again. In fact they had been outposts, and were pitted against the influence of the Association clubs which threatened to sweep them out of existence, and he thought it was quite time the Rugby Union looked at it in that light. lie compli- mented the New Zealand team on the splendid game they put up against Warrington that afternoon, as they knew Warrington had a fine record, having been winners of the Northern Union cup twice and many other cups. He admired the New Zealanders for playing a fine manly game in a sportsmanlike manner, and for the way they had taken their defeat. (Applause.) He coupled with the toast the name of Mr. " Bumper " Wright. The toast was drunk with musical honours and cheers.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. BASKER,VILLE'S TRIBUTE.

Mr. BASKERVILLE, the organiser of the tour, responded in the absence of Mr. Wright, who had been called away. He said that with regard to the relative strength of the last " All Blacks " and the present team, the present team had an opinion upon that point. (Hear, hear.) They were not better men, but if they, were to play the same team, they would back them- selves every day in the week. (Laughter.) The game with Warrington had been a very good one, but as they all knew, the New Zealanders were ' dry weather players," and the ground was not to their liking. He did not say that, to depreciate in the least, Warrington's performance, but if the ground had been dry, not only would the " All Blacks" have done better, but he expected that Warrington would have done better also. Still the game was a good one— (applause)—and he could say that the New Zealanders had enjoyed it. He must compliment the Secretary and Treasurer upon the excellent ground appointments. He thought they were about the best he had seen so far. (Applause.) He also wished to refer to Mr. Limley's handling of the game. Both sides were satis- fied—(applause)—and it was not often that. they got an official who so well satisfied both. He (Mr. Basker- ville) thanked Warrington for the excellent reception the team had received at the Town Hall, at the ground, and at that hotel. It W 49 a pity they could not give some return for the generous way they had been received. He also thanked the club for the entertain- ment next day, and if ever a team went over to New Zealand, and it contained Warrington representatives, he hoped they would have revenge and send them back as satisfied as they (the New Zealand team; would be on Monday morning. (Applause.) MR. GLEESON'S PICTURESQUE SPEECH.

Mr. GLMESON, in proposing " The Warrington Club," said the Sew Zealand team had experienced such splendid hospitality in the great industrial centres of the North of England that they would never forget it. (Applause.) The team appreciated the kindness of the club, and he would say that from the first thy at Folkestone, when they felt the hand of friendship cordially extended to them, they had felt it to he a proud privilege to be members of the British Empire. (Applause.) And when further, they visited Westmios ter Abbey and saw there the tombs of the Kings of England, and bannerettea of kings and nobles and worthies, it was indeed a pleasure to come from a country where the parents of some of them had been the first to strike the woodman's axe into the bosh since Creation. They felt it was a privilege and a distinction to have landed on the shores of old England. (Applause.) He could only say that they could have wished to have won more victories in the Northern Union. Warrington was well-known in New Zealand as the place from which they exported to that Colony, that sterling article, the wire which they got to keep out the rabbits. (Laughter.) He wished they could have taken back the ashes of victory so that they could fan to the flame the cause of Northern Unionism from one end of the Australasian Colonies to the other. (Applause.) It was a great pleasure and gratification to observe gentlemen like Sir GilLert Greenall taking such interest in football. (Applause.) fie could only endorse what was said by Mr. Baskerville, that if any Warrington representatives accompanied a Northern Union team to New Zealand they would do all they could to treat them with similar hospitality as they were receiving that evening. (Applause.) The members of the team would in drinking the toast sing in the old Maori tongue "For he's a jolly good fellow." The Maoris were the finest native race in the world, and when the British conquered New Zealand about 80 years ago they made an indelible impression upon their conquerers. (Applause.) The visitors after drinking the toast sang " For he's a jolly good fellow" in the Maori tongue, and gave three Maori cheers.

INFANTILE MORTALITY.

Canon Scott Holland on the Sacredness of Child Life.

Canon Scott Holland preached at Christmas time from the words, "A Little child shall lead them " (Isaiah xi. 6). He said that God made His eternal name known to mankind,on Christmas Day through

THE BA.BYHOOD OF JESUS. He hoped to be understood by us, to make manifest what He meant by Elis Fatherhood, through laying in the arms of a human mother a babe as helpless and more pure than any of our owe. There it lay, and that was all. Its babyhood was enough for Christmas Day. In its helplessness it made a revelation which the Father desired, and which our hearts could follow. Drawn to the babe by its soft, sleeping innocence, we were drawn nearer to God than we had ever been before. No babe of our own had ever doubted the goodness of man towards it, and by virtue of this entire refusal to doubt man, man actually became better. That was what we saw happening about us through the chil- dren, and now God drew us to goodness by this same compulsion through the newborn babe, Jesus. lie put Himself at our mercy, and trusted to our compassion. He simply placed in our midst that helpless Child and suffered it to do upon us its wondrous work. Simply to see that soft, sleeping body the big world must for ever come to some pause in its rough businesses. Shepherds left their sheep for a time and forgot the peril of the wolf, wise men looked up from their books, and kings ceased their ruling. All the carking care and soaring ambitions of this world became things of little import- ance as we knelt at the inang3r and watched the Child. Was there not some secret fount of joy behind and beyond it all? That joy which we had sought elsewhere and never found was here. It was found in giving ourselves to the service of that which was weak and helpless and which had been committed to our keaoln, And as we , knelt and looked at the Holy Babe, should we nat remember with

===== 0002601_19071228_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
BURNIKTI SHAME

those babies that to-day, all over England, we handed over to the martyrdom of neglect ? tie had said that morning that this noisy world would stay its wrangling at the sight of a child's face. So it would, if it saw it, but what if it never noticed it, if it forgot it. if it pissed by on the other side? That was what was at the botcorn of all our terrible tale of infant mortality. We suffered at least 100 babies out of every 1,000 in the poorer districts of London to perish without natural causes because they were out of sight. We wonld not look that way • we refused to consider it. Not only in the slums of London, but in the rich industrial centres of this country. tonow booming with trade—in Lancashire and Yorkshire—the holocaust of children was being piled up week by week because the mothers were all out at work in factories keeping up our wealth, an.l the roaring wheels rushed round while the CHILDREN' PERISHED FOR LACK OF MOTHERHOOD,

..... _ and because they were denied the God-ordained boon of mother's milk. If we saw that, and it was really under our eyes, we should not tolerate it for an hour. Let our eyes fasten themselves on the horrible statistics of infantile mortality ; let us look at each starving babe as we gazed at the Babe born for us in Bethlehem, and then vow our Christmas vow before God's altar that, in the name of Jesus, we would never rest while babes were born in England only to be unsuckled and misfed; that we would never rest until the glad Christmas came when every babe born in England should at least find prepared for its true and ,proper heritage the primal ministries of motherhood and the elemental humanities of home.

===== 0002601_19071228_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
OLD BUT EVER NEW.

To Britishers of to-day, young or old, at home or abroad, there is no more familiar every day name than " Mother Seigel's Syrop." Forty years of public use have made this famous remedy, literally and truly "a household word." The secret of the long continued popularity of Mother Seigel's Syrup lies in two facts: First, it has never been held forth as a cure-all," but solely as a cure for indigestion, biliousness, and the ailments that arise from derangements of the digestive system. Next, it is a vegetable preparation, and when used to correct the irreguhrities of stomach, liver or bowels, the contingent ailments, such as headache, wind, dizziness, also disappear. It will doubtless interest many of our readers to learn that this standard medicine is also now sold in new form of tablets, under the name of " Mother Seigel's Syrup Tablets." Their action is said to be precisely that of the Syrup, and they should prove very convenient and desirable to people when travelling, or when away from home in the ordinary course of business. They are put up in neat and haady packages, suitable for the waist- coat pocket or a lady's hai.d sachel.

A CHAPTER OF LOCAL HISTORY.

THE YEAR 1907 REVIEWED.

As we stand on the threshold of a new year it is interesting to take stock of the events by which the past twelve months will be chiefly remembered locally. On the whole 1907 has not been a remarkable year, as far as Warrington is concerned, but. there have been some happenings of importance which will occupy a prominent place in the history of our borough. In the political world, while interest has not run so high as in the previous year which witnessed the general election, enthusiasm and activity have by no means declined. The progressive ranks are as well organized as ever and the Liberal meetings held during the year have afforded the electors every opportunity of keeping in touch with the great reforms which the Government has already achieved. Early in the year—on February Ist—we were honoured with a visit for the first time for many years from a Cabinet Minister in the person of Mr. Sydney Buxton, M.P., the Postmaster General. The organization of this great meeting which took place in the Parr Hall was the special work of the Junior Liberals who were cordially congratulated upon its success. On February 17th Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P. attended another big , meeting in the same hall and gave an account of his stewardship as Warrington's representative at West- minster, outlining in a clear and convincing way the principal measures undertaken by the Government. , The Liberal agents of Lancashire and Cheshire held I their conference at Warrington on April 22nd and in the evening they were the guests at tea of Mr. Crosfield who in an excellent address dealt chiefly with the isse.sonne/ of the Ministry. On June 27th Warrington progressives were delighted to learn; that the Borough Member had delivered his maiden speech in the House the previous evening on the far- reaching question of the veto of the House of Lords. I Mr. Crosfield's remarks were well-chosen and the House accorded him a hearty reception, while the speech gave the greatest satisfaction to his constitu- ents. The members of the various local Liberal organizations spent a very happy half-day on September 21st when a picnic to Belle \rue took place and proved an unqualified success. The Member accompanied the party and gave a capital address on current politics. ' Commencing on October 25th, Mr. Crosfield fulfilled a series of engagements in Warrington and district , including meetings of the Junior Liberals aud Pro- gressive Labour League, and his addresses dealt most , ably with the burning questions of the day. While on political affairs, reference must be made to a ques- tion of great interest to all parties which came up at the Revision Court on September 10th. The recently appointed Revising Barrister (Mr. C. H. M. Wharton) gave the important decision that in future he would , not sanction compromises arrived at by the party agents in the matter of allowing votes, but would in all cases require proof that the claimants were entitled to the franchise. A social event in which Warrington was greatly interested was the marriage of Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P. to Miss Domini Elliadi • which took place in Paris on December 2nd. The happy couple have , been the recipients of warm congratulations, numerous ' telegrams expressing good wishes being forwarded from Warrington on the day of the wedding. In the realm of municipal administration the year has been an unusually peaceful one, thanks in a large measure to the tactful conduct of the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) in the chair. One of the special subjects which occupied attention in the earlier part of the year was the preservation of the open space adjoining the Training College in Manchester- road. The Council acceded to the general wish of the residents and workers in the Ward, as expressed in a very numerously signed petitim, with the result that the necessary strip of land was purchased from the trustees of the Clergy Widows and Orphans Fund for £3,000. The prolonged litigation between the traders of Warrington and the Ship Canal Company ended in a great victory for the traders on May 29th, the [louse of Lords upholding the judgment of the late Mr. Justice Byrne, by which the Company were compelled to fulfil their statutory obligations to preserve a channel of a depth of Bft. at low water of. spring tides. The Cor- poration and traders were represented in opposition to the Ship Canal Bill which was passed in June with clauses Rafoguttrding Warrington with regord te salinity of water used for boiler purposes, the preserva- tion of public footpath rights on Arpley meadows, and in other respecp.The much-vexed Lawson Contract gave rise to a long and heated discussion at the June meeting of the Council,but in the endthe Council sanctioned the appointment of Mr. George Hill, of Manchester, to arbitrate on the claim. The arbitration proceedings took place in London during the week commencing October 21st, but notwithstanding a petition signed by the majority of the Council in favour of publicity, the Press were excluded from the hearing. The arbitrator's reward has not yet been received. The question of widening Warrington Bridge still remains unsettled. At the meeting of the Council in July the Mayor introduced a resolution that the Surveyor be instructed to prepare specifications for a ferro-concrete bridge to take the place of the present structure and also for a new bridge at Howley, but differences of opinion were manifested and the pro- position was withdrawn. It is believed, however, that a scheme will be introduced in the near future. A. welcome feature oF the Council's work was the reduction of the rates by 4d, making the present amount 7s. At the May meeting the Finance Com- mittee brought forward a recommendation that a pro- fessional auditor be appointed to audit the Borough Accounts but after discussion the question was deferred, and has not been re-introduced. On October sth one of the present elective auditors (Alr. E. England) issued a lengthy report in which he com- mended the financial adtninistrationof the Borough but passed some strong comments on the health of the town and made an indictment against property owners. During the year it has been the duty of the Council to fill vacancies caused by the lamented death of two prominent Corporation officials ; Mr. Martin Nicholls, of Windsor, being appointed Chief Con- stable, and Mr. J. Coote Hibbert, of Newcastle-on- Tyne, medical officer of heilth. At the municipal elections in November there were only nye contasts in Howley and Town Hall Wards —and in each instance a candidate connected with "the trade" was returned. As a result, there has since been a strong movement for the formation of a Temperance Electoral Council to check the ascendancy of "the trade" in public life. To the general satisfaction of the towns- people, Alderman Slethurst was at the annual meet- ing of the Council in November re-elected Mayor, the urbane and impartial manner in which he had dis- charged the duties during the preceding twelve months having won for him the respect and esteem of all parties. Councillor Ellison was elected to the aldermanic seat rendered vacant by the resignation of the late Mr. EL W.Francorub. A long discussion took place on a suggested alteration of the seniority rule, and it was eventually decided to adhere to the principle of recognizing a Councillor's previous services, whether interrupted or not, in determining seniority. Latterly the question of the public health has come much to the fore, and at the December Council meeting Coun- cillor Evans, as Chairman of the Health C ominittee, submitted an import int statement on the questions of infantile mortality, slum property, the smoke nuisance, and the suggested public abattoir. The Rev. Sidney Pitt, the superintendent minister of the Warrington Wesleyan circuit, is greatly interesting himself in these matters, and has suggested the formation of a voluntary association to co-operate with the health authority with the object of dispelling the ignorance which must be an important contributory factor to the high death rate. A Local Government Board Inquiry of more than usual interest took place on January 22nd, the subject being the proposed new street from iSankey Street to Rylande-street. So strong was the !opposition that the proposal ti construet a street of la uniform width of 40 feet was abandoned.

-As in the past the Education Committee has pur- sued its curse, free from any unfortunate biekerings on the religious question, and some interesting and important topics have come up for discussion. At the January meeting there was a long debate on the le-naming of People's College, which was insisted upon by the Education Department in London. The name proposed was" The 13rewtnall School," but the Committee as a whole did not favour the recommenda- tion, and at a subsequent meeting it wa4 agreed to ,call the institution the " Arpley-street School." The cost of education continues on the un-grade, the estimates approved in February involving a call on the rates of is. 41d., as compared with is. 3d. for the previous year. Warrington's first Council School— the Bearnont School -was formally opened on March 22nd by Alderman Bennett, in the absence of Lord Stanley of Alderley. The school is constructed on the most modern lines, and is replete with upto-date equipment. At the June meeting of the Committee a syllabus of Bible instruction for Council schools was approved. The foundation stone of a second Council

school—the Bolton school which will serve the Latch- ford district —was laid by the Mayor on July sth, and the building is nearing completion. Mr. C. B. Aylward having resigned the headmastership of the School of Art, the School Management Committee on June 21st appointed Mr. Clarence Mawson, A..R.C.A., of Shipley, to succeed him. Some discussion ensued with regard to the claims of a local candidate, but Mr. Mawson's appointment was con- firmed by the Education Committee. The feeding of school children was the subject of debate at the September meeting of the committee and it wa9 eventually decided to apply to the Board of Education for permission to adopt the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1900. An interesting project which has received consideration lately is the provision of a new school and recreation ground on a vacant plot of land off Lovely-lane, and at the November meeting of the Committee it was decided to recommend the Town Council to purchase seven acres for that purpose subject to the Board of Education's approval. On October 30th the governors of the Grammar School ' unanimously appointed the Rev. Horace Gray, M.A., headmaster of the Kendal Grammar School, to succeed the late Rev. E. J. Willcocks as headmaster. Mr. Gray, in addition to possessing notable scholastic qualifications, is a keen cricketer, having won his " blue " at Cambridge. The year 1907 has been one of great moment in the history of the Warrington Infirmary. For some years the need for additional accommodation has been mani- fest and so generously has the scheme been supported that on January 31st the foundation stone of this most necessary extension was laid, Lord Lilford performing the ceremony in the presence of a very representative i gathering. The nurses' home which forms part of the new building is already in occupation and the opening of the additional wards is expected to take place in the early spring. In the meantime an urgent appeal is made for the balance of 3:1,250 required in order that the extension may be opened entirely free I from debt. The great effort of the year was the bazaar which took place at Hill Cliffe in June and was the means of adding £2,271 trw the fund.

In the religious world one of the outstanding events has been the centenary of the Primitive Methodist Church which was duly celebrated at Legh-street Church by a series of meetings in January. At the first gathering on January 17th Mr. J. Whitley, of Stockton Heath,gave an interesting account of the early days of the denomination. At the annual tea meeting in connection with St. Paul's Church, which was held in the Parr Hall on January 28th, the Rev. W. Brace- camp was the recipient of a token of esteem in the form of an illuminated address —the gift of the church officers. On Spday,March 17th one of the best-attended services for men ever held in Warrington took place at the Parish Church, the preacher being Canon Scott Holland, of St. Paul's Cathedral. On March 31st, the Rev. J. S. Hughes, having resigned the pastorate of Hill Cliff° Baptist Church, preached his farewell sermon and was the recipient of a presentation from , the congregation. On April 21st Bold-street Wesleyan Church was re-opened after decoration. On April 28th , Mr. John Kirk, now Sir John Kirk, visited War- rington to take part in the celebration of the twentieth anniversary of the Ragged School and his touching narratives of work amongst the poverty-stricken created a deep impression. The question of the incorpora- tion of the Connexional Committee created a division in the Independent Methodist Church, and simul- taneously with the annual meetings of the Connexion at Bristol on June 21st, the churches whiGh dissented from incorporation held meetings at Friars Green Church, Warrington, and passed a resolution defining their position. The Rev. C. L. Stowe, the energetic minister of Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church, left Warrington for Eccles in June, his farewell sermon being preached on June 23rd. He has been succeeded by the Rev. J. 8.. Buglass. July 11th was a red letter day in the history of Bold-street Wesleyan Church, the fine new organ which has been installed at a cost of .t9OO being opened by Dr. J. Kendrick Pyne. On August 15th the Rev. J. Manning, who had worked most successfully at Bold-street for three years, preached his farewell sermon on leaving for Preston. He was succeeded by the Rev. T. Miller who preached his first sermon at Bold-street on September Ist. On October 18th the corner stone of the new church of St. Margaret, Orford, was laid with Masonic rites by Lord Lathom in the presence of a large gathering. Wesleyanism is making gratifying progress at Latchford and a commodious new Sunday School was formerly opened by Mr. John Taylor on November 28th. The latest change in local clerical 'circles is the appointment of the Rev.W. Pollock-Hill, Vicar of Christ Church, Padgate to the living of St. Thomas', Ashton-in-Makerfield, which was announced on December 10th.

The Beautiful Warrington Society eontinues to do a good work in improving the appearance of the town by planting trees and encouraging the cultivation of flowers. It is especially pleasing that the work amongst the rising generation is making great headway, and the school children's bulb show held on March 23rd was a great success, The annual show promoted by the Society was held at the Drill Hall on August 31st and proved a noteworthy advance on last year's display. The opening ceremony was per- forme 1 by Mrs. Lyon, of Appleton Hall, whose speech was particularly interesting and well chosen. At the annual meeting of the Society on October 3rd., some strong things were said about the smoke nuisance and there is reason to believe that measures will ere long be taken to mitigate the evil. Another movement closely allied with the beautiful Warrington S3ciety although having no direct connection with it, is the Garden Suburbs Schema. A company has been formed and has secured in addition to a 20 acre plot on the Liverpool- road, a second piece of 22 acres at Morris Brook, Latchford, and it is hoped soon to establish the first suburb at Sankey.

The year 1907 Will be long remembered as witnessing the visit to Warrington of General Sir Redvers Buller, V.C. The veteran warrior came here to unveil the memorial erected in Queen's Gardens to the South Lancashire soldiers who fell in the South African cam- paign, and scenes of enthusiasm were witnessed the like of which it would be difficult to recall. The cere- mony itself was deeply impressive and all were touched by Sir Redvers' obviously heartfelt tribute to the men who died whilst serving under his command. It is highly fitting that Warrington should have a memorial to John Howard, the great prison reformer, who was closely associated with town, and on April sth a suitable tablet which has been affixed to Howard Buildings in Bridge-street, at the instance of the Warrington Society, was unveiled by Major Arthur Griffiths.

the great event of the year in the realm of sport was the remarkable success of the Warrington Football team which; for the second time in six years, won the Northern Union Cup by defeatinc, Oldham on April 27t1a after a stiff struggle at Broughton. The victory aroused the utmost enthusiasm in the town and the footballers were handsomely entertained by Sir Gilbert °Toenail at Walton Hall on May 17th, and by Mr. A. H. Crosaeld, M.P., at Chester on the following day. At the annual meeting of the club on May 21st the treasurer (Mr. T. Pe nberton) was able to report a great improvement in the financial standing and an increase in memllership. The present season will rank as memorable by reason of the visit on Saturday last of the' New Zealand team Which produced a splendidly- fought encounter, the local lads triumphing by the odd point. The cricket season of 1907 was on the whole a successful one for the Warrington eleven, but it was a great disappointment to them not to secure the championship of the West Lancashire League. St. Helens proved too good for them and carried off the trophy, Warrington being second in the League. Individual, members of the eleven did splendidly in the competition matches, J. W. Bibby winning the gold medal for the best batting average and Cresswell, the professional, a similar award for the best bowling analysis. C. Berry, Warrington's crack cyclist, carried off the 60 guinea challenge cup at New- town on June 29th in the three miles scratch race. Et is gratifying to note that the movement for the pro- vision of recreative facilities by firms for their employees continues to spread. On April 19th a bowl- ing green and ride range provided for the amusement of the local workers on the London and North-Western Railway were opened by Mr. and Mrs. Prank Hee, an interesting social gathering taking place in the Assembly Room at the Conservative Club.

%Va.rrifigton people hoard with pleasure early in January that Mr. J. Rymer Young, one of our best known townsmen, had been elected President of the Pharmaceutical Society of Great Britain—an honour which he has undoubtedly earned by his great interest in the Society. At the beginning of May, Mr. I. T. Williams, the exceedingly popular district goods manager of the L. and N.-W. Railway, left Warrington to take up the more responsible position of superinten- dent of the London goods stations and of the Goods and Parcels Agency, London. He has been succeeded by Mr. F. A. Sargent, of London. Warrington has come to the fore in the musical sense of late years and many gratifying successes have been achieved by local competitors at mislead festivals in England and %Vales. Messrs. Crosfleld's Prize Band enjoyed an extraordinary run of guccesses and on May 25th won the first prize consisting of ,C3O, a silver. mounted baton and a challenge cup value 50 guineas, at New Brighton in competition with fourteen other bands.

While referring to matters musical, mention must be made of the fine performance of Elgar's magnificent work "The Dream of Gerontius " which was given by the Warrington Musical Society on November 2Gth. The public patronage was remarkable and, we hope, was significant of an awakening of much greater interest in

the work of the Musical Society than has hitherto bees manifested.

at the meeting of the Co-operative Society in July the resignation of Mr. A. H. Rathbone, who had acted as secretary and manager for many years,was announced, and testimony was paid to the value of Mr. Rathbone's devoted service. The Society has since appointed Mr. J. Jarman as secretary and Mr. Atkinson as manager. Warrington is now exceptionally well catered for in regard to entertainments. The Royal Come- Theatre, which was burnt down on Christmas Day, 1906, was re-opened on Monday, August sth, by Mr. William Mollison and his company in "Beside the Bonnie Brier Bush." The foundation stone of the Palace and Hippodrome at Friars' Gate was laid by the Mayor on May lst,and the building was opened on September 23rd- On July 17th a disastrous fire occured at the works of Messrs. R. A. N'aylor, Ltd.,timber merchants,Howley, and damage to the extent of £20,000 was done. Events of particular interest to school children were the celebration of Empire Day on May 17th (the Mayor and Mayoress visiting all the schools and presenting medals and brooches to the children selected by the votes of their fellow-scholars) and Trafalgar Day which was observed on October 21st by the presentation of prizes given by Alderman Bennett for the best essays on Colonel O'Leary and John Howard. Unfortunately the death roll for the year includes many of our most highly respected townsmen. On January 31st, Mr. W. R. Neil, director of Fletcher. Russell and Co., Ltd., and for three years president of the Warrington Football Club, passed away. The funeral took place at Hill Cliffe on January 26th of Mr. Thomas Hall, of Statham, Lymm, a former well-known Warrington tradesman and a prominent Baptist. On February 4th, Dr. J. Guest Gornall, who had served the town devotedly as medical officer of health since 1901, passed away. On February 7th the death occurred of Mr. John Clarke, an old inhabitant, and on February 14th,of Mr.Williarn Atherton,manager of the engineering department at Dallam Forge. The obituary for this month also included Mrs. J. R. Buckton who died on February 22nd. On March 7th news was received of the tragic end of Mr. Luke Talbot, Chief Constable' at Cairo, and created a painful sensation. Mr. John W. Roberts, of Wilderspool, passed away on the 22nd; Mr. William Forsiaaw, member of the firm of Messrs. E. Forshaw and Son, and Mr. William Cummins, a well- known bandsman, on the 30th ; ex-Councillor T. Wright and Mr. T.G. Grounds on the 31st. Mr. Josiah Whittle,a well-known tradesman, died on April sth, and the Rev. S. Wilkinson' Vicar of Halton and formerly Vicar of St. Paul's, Warrington, on April 24th. Mr. L. W.. Reynolds passed away with painful suddenness on April 30th. During May the obituary included Mr. George Jolley and Mr. John T. Blease on the 2nd; Mr. Peter Caldwell, founder of the firm of Caldwell Ltd., on the 4th ; Mr.E.W. Clarke, of Penketh, and Mrs. Kidd on the 13th. Mrs. Elizabeth Truslove passed away on June 11th; Mr. G. L. Devvhurst, of Lymm, on July 26th; Mr. W. G. Bennett, of Garstang, eldest son of Mr. John Bennett, Grange Mount, Padget°, on July 27th; Mr. W. Bede Houghton, on the 28th. Great regret was occa- sioned, particularly in Volunteer circles, by the death • on August 7th of Colonel J. C. Ridgway, and the town lost another of its most honoured public workers on ; August 15th in the person of the Rev. E. J. Willcocks, headmaster of the Boteler Grammar School since 1880. ' Roman Catholics heard with regret that the Rev. J. L McGrath, for five years priest-in-charge of St. Mary's, Latchford, died on August 14th. Mrs. L. W. Reynolds passed away on October 7th, and Mr. Thomas Gandy on " October 14th. The town suffered a double loss in the first week of the present month by the death on December Ist of Alderman James Fairclough and on December 3rd of Mr. R. W. Francomb, both ex-Mayors of the Borough. The year now drawing to a close has been a pros- . perous one for local trade, and the workers in most branches of manufacture appear to have been kept busy. We hope that the coming year will be one of even ' greater success for the town and its numerous L industries.

THE ALL BLACKS VISIT.

WARRINGTON WIN A FINE MATCH. —.— Desperate Struggle at Wilderspool.

By " Ovallst."

The All Blacks have been and lost! Warrington were determined to emulate the feats of Wigan, Oldham, and the others who have triumphed over the New Zealand footballers, and after a great struggle on Saturday they just won by 8 points to 7. It was. a score which represented pretty accurately the merits of the teams. While Warrington were superior in combination and effectiveness among the backs and had a larger share of the game, the All Blacks held a big advantage forward and played with great keen- ness. Their defence in the second half was superb, and nothing more thrilling has been seen on the Warrington ground for a long time than that desperate battle near the New Zealand line which existed for a solid twenty minutes and only came to an end when Taylor burst through and gave Warrington the winning point. Time after time it seemed as, if Warrington must score, but the tackling of the. New Zealanders was deadly and the brine attack just a little flurried through excitement. Ido not think the New Zealanders have previously had such a long speil on the defence, and it speaks volumes for the solidity of the home ranks and the pushfulness of the attackers, that the All Blacks only reached the home quarter twice during the second half of the game. The first half was really the best from a spectatorial point of view, for the ball travelled from end to end in rapid style, and the handling and running on both sides were high- class. Fish opened the score after a characteristic movement on the Warrington left. The ball was sent out near centre, and though Dickenson did Some clever feinting for position, the chances of a try did not seem great when Fish received. There was a crowd of New Zealand defenders swarming round that side of the field, bat the Warrington crack swerved through at lightning speed and got in at the corner in miraculous fashion. Warrington were going great guns at this stage, their pace and cleverness com- pletely baffling the All Blacks. Thomas and Dickenson were both within inches of penetrating the defence and another of Fish's electrifying efforts was nipped in the bud by a timely but reprehensible trip. Thomas kicked a penalty goal, and then the New Zealanders had a look in. Messenger evoked wondering cheers with a magnificent goal from the touch-line at half-way—and this success seemed to stimulate the visitors. They set up a brilliant attack which culminated in Messenger racing. over at the corner and round the posts, the same versatile player again goaling with ease. Warrington were thus in arrears at the interval and they did not get ahead again until about eight minutes from time. It was a do-or-die contest, and though honours were pretty evenly • divided, the best side won. Bravo Warrington ! The outstanding figure of the match, despite his modest inches, was that of H. S. Turtill, the New Zealand full back. He had an immense amount of work, but he came through without a single errer, and was the very personification of safety and resource. Turtill does not love the Northern Union touch rule with its limitation of the full back's defensive measures, and thinks it wonld be an improvement if the full back, or whoever had fallen back to that position, had the option of finding touch direct. Nevertheless the New Zealand custodian maintains a fine defence even under NAT. rules, and his punting is invariably powerful and well placed. On Saturday he gave a perfect exhibition. Next to him in point of individual excellence comes Dickenson, who played a superb centre game for Warrington. The only failures were Lees (who was very slow in getting the ball to the home backs) and Tyler, who was out of place on the New Zealand wing. Messenger was very clever and tricky, and Todd worked like a Trojan at five-eighths. G. W. Smith was a much-missed man at centre three-quarter, and the New Zealand side will be severely handicapped until he returns. In my opinion they would do better with Messenger at centre or five-eighths than on the wing. He and Rowe might have changed places with advantage on Satur- day. When the New Zealanders were winning every match at the beginning of the tour the forwards were hopelessly beaten for possession. Now, when the team is only winning about two matches out of three, the forwards are almost invincible. It is a most curious fact. In theory the All Blacks ought to be unbeietable now that the forwards have learnt to apply their great strength and speed in the right direction, but the backs are finding men of equal calibre opposed to them, and men, too, *ho are wide awake to their sudden and often original method of attack. The details of the match are as foPlow :

Rain fell persistently in the morning and this doubt- less affected the attendance. Nevertheless there was a capital crowd of 8,000, and the receipts, including ticket money, amounted to £3BO. Luckily for the match, the rain ceased about one o'clock and kept off for the rest of the day. Owing to its good covering of grav, the ground was in fair order though very soft. Promptly to time the Warrington men came on the field, followed by the All Blacks, who received a great cheer of welcome. The local players gave three "hurrahs "in midfield and the tourists responded with their dramatic war-chant, in which Bumper" Wright has become as fine a leader of the chorus as he is captain of the side. The teams then lined up in the following order _

Warriniton—Tilley, back; Fish, Diceknson, Taylor, and Brooks, three-quarter backs ; Lees (captain) and Jenkins, half-backs; Shugars, Thomas,Boardman,Belton, Harmer' and Naylor, forwards. New Zealand—Turtill, back; Messenger, Rowe, and Tyler, three-quarter backs • Todd and Wrigley, five- eighths; R. Wynyard, half back: Wright (captain), Trevarthen, Pearce, Gilchrist, Cross, and Byrne, for- wards..

Referee, Mr. J. H. Lumley, Leeds. Touch judges, Messrs. W. H. Cawrey, Runcorn, and J. Moore, Oldham. Thomas started for Warrington against the wind. Exchange kicking between the backs ended in Wynyard kicking well down the field. Offside by the home forwards gave the visitors a footing inside the home half. Messenger assayed a drop at goal, hut the effort was a poor one, Tilley touching down. From the drop out Warrington forwards endeavoured to rush matters,- but Wrigley stopped the movement. Boardman, one of the home forwards, met with a slight injury and left the field for a few minutes. Wynyard got away from the scrimmage removing play to the home quarter line. Fish returned promptly with a fine dribble but the Warrington flyer was tripped by Tyler when well away. The referee promptly ordered a penalty, but the shot at goal by Thomas was a poor one. Wrigley brought relief by a kick to midfield. Tilley was not caught napping for he made a prompt reply. The Warrington backs indulged in a pretty passing movement, Taylor and Brooks being especially prominent. The latter was sprinting along touch when he was bowled over by Tartill. Another bout of passing by the home backs ended in Dickenson drawing Tyler on to him, and then banding on to Fish who, although surrounded by a host of players, dashed over the line at the corner, amidst tremendous cheering. Thomas failed to land the goal. This success put new life into the homesters, who raided the visitors' half. Thomas headed a forward dribble, which was stopped on the line by Wriglgy. At this stage the New Zealanders were experiencing a very rough time in keeping the Wirepullers from scoring. The homesters were playing a great game, and Dickenson inter- cepting ran clean through. When almost over he was smartly tackled from behind by Wrigley under the post. A free kick for offside was here turned to good account by Thomas, the ball being sent safely between the posts. Fish Welded a high kick from Rowe with splendid judgment. He swerved past Tyler and was careering for the line when the whistle blew for an informality, one of the home players being at fault. A free kick was given from which Messenger kicked a grand goal from near the half-way line, and close to touch. The feat was warmly applauded. A kick by Jenkins was smartly caught by Messenger, who dashed away at top speed,but Brooks, who was exceedingly nippy, brought him down before he had travelled very far. A smart passing bout between Wyeyard, Todd, and Wrigley was stopped by G. Dickenson. With the aid of a free kick the All Blacks gained a footing in the Warrington quarter. The forwards dribbled close to the line, when Taylor was cheered for a smart save. Continuing the pressure the All Blacks handled the slippery ball with splendid judgment. From a grand passing bout Messenger dashed over, completing a brilliant effort with a try behind the posts. The same player had no difficulty iu converting. With two points in arrears the Wirepullers resumed in a vigorous fashion, Dickenson executing a brilliant run takiag play beyond the half-way line. Turtill was deservedly applauded for a fine clearaace when hotly pressed by the home forwards. By short dribbles the visitors got down to the Warrington end, a free kick to Warrington bringing relief.

HALF-TIME.

A VICTIM OF THE FOG. Pathetic Drowning Case at Stockton Heath.

--- Christmastide has been marked by an exceptionally- pathetic drowning case at Stockton Heath. During the dense fog which prevailed on Monday evening, Jane Roberts, aged sixty-one, who resided at 14 Greenbank, ‘Vilderspool, set out to meet her husband, who was returniug from work at Acton Grange. A neighbour tried to dissuade her from going along the bank of the Manchester Ship Canal but she was uneasy about her husband and persisted in going that way. She had only traversed a short distance when she missed her way and fell into the caual. Her husband took the pre- caution, owing to the fog, to return home by the main road, and as he was walking home he heard the piercing screams of his wife, but was unable to render any assist- ance. Grappling operations were commenced and the body was recovered on Tuesday morning. Mr. R. Dobson, coroner, held the inquaat at tbe Stag Inn, Lower Walton, on Christmas Eve.—Captain Waring appeared for the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Robert Roberts, a lock gate man employed by the Ship Canal Company, said deceased was his wife. He last saw her alive at 6.40 on Monday morning when he left home to go to work. He told her he would be working late, and would not be home until 6.35 in the evening. Ile generally walked along the north side of the canal to and from work and then crossed by the ferry, and his wife knew of that. As it was very foggy on Monday evening he walked round by Ellesmere- road. As be was going home he heard a woman calling for help from the canal, but could do nothing. On reaching home he found the doors locked, and he went to the north side of the canal and started grappling operations, for he was sure it must be his wife who was in the water. She had been down the north side of the canal in daylight but had never come to meet him before She was rather short-sighted. It was so foggy that witness had a lamp to guide him to the Stag Bridge. Peter Sayer, who lived next door to the deceased, stated that on Monday night Mrs. Roberts became very anxious about her husband on account of his non- arrival. Ho advised her not to go along the canal bank, as he thought her husband would return along Ellesmere-road instead of by the canal bank. Peter Ward, 14, Litherland's Yard,Old Road, Warring- ton, said he was at Acton Grange that morning. and at 10.15 he saw the body of the deceased floating face doTnwards in the Ship Canal. He got help and recovered the body. Deceased was grasping a key in each hand.

The jury returned a verdict of " Accidentally drowned" and on their behalf the foreman (Mr. William Weir) expressed deep sympathy with the husband. -

PADGATE.

SUSDAN SCHOOL SOCEAL.—The elder scholars of the Padgate Church of England School and their friends held a Christmas social in the Schoolroom on Friday evening. The room was tastefully decorated with garlands, Chinese lanterns and evergreens and there was a large attendance. Mr. F. Turner sang " The flight of ages" and "The Holy City" in excellent style. Hiss L. Ross gives very sweet rendering of "A dream of home." Hiss R. Itighey proved herself a very capable vocalist her songs "We'd better bide a.vee " and "The gates • of the west " being applauded. Dancing was kept up with zest,the playing or Miss Gladys Ross, the pianist, giving every satisfaction. All the arrangements were efficiently carried out by Mr. G. W. Rigbey, the Superin. tendent.
